# New universality classes associated to fractals

**arXiv ID**: 2607.28379v1
**Authors**: Benjamin Eichinger, Alexander Kheifets, Milivoje Lukić, Peter Yuditskii
**Published**: 2026-07-30
**Categories**: math.SP, math-ph, math.CA
**Comments**: 63 pages
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.28379v1

## Abstract

The local behavior of zeros of orthogonal polynomials is determined by the local scaling behavior of Christoffel-Darboux (CD) kernels. All previously studied behaviors are described by scaling limits, and different values of the limit kernel correspond to different universality classes. In this paper, we describe new universality classes in which instead of a single limit kernel, there is a limit cycle. These are naturally suited to Cantor spectra and to fractal behaviors of the measure. We show that these new phenomena occur for two canonical models with singular measures: the middle third Cantor measure and the balanced/equilibrium measure on a real Julia set of an expanding polynomial. In particular, this is the first result on the local behavior of CD kernels for an almost periodic operator with singular spectrum.   As a complementary result, we describe the asymptotics of the scaling function, and the Christoffel function, at a fixed point of a quadratic iteration. This is the first such analysis for an almost periodic model with singular spectrum. It allows us to conclude that, at the fixed point, the local scaling of zeros of the polynomial of degree $n$ is precisely of order $n^{-1/α}$, where $α$ is the local dimension of the measure. We also study the limit chain in this case, and prove that it can be parametrized by its asymptotics with respect to a Martin function (so-called $M$-type); this is the first result of this kind for a chain with a singular measure.

## Abstract.

The local behavior of zeros of orthogonal polynomials is determined by the local scaling behavior of Christoffel–Darboux (CD) kernels. All previously studied behaviors are described by scaling limits, and different values of the limit kernel correspond to different universality classes. In this paper, we describe new universality classes in which instead of a single limit kernel, there is a limit cycle. These are naturally suited to Cantor spectra and to fractal behaviors of the measure. We show that these new phenomena occur for two canonical models with singular measures: the middle third Cantor measure and the balanced/equilibrium measure on a real Julia set of an expanding polynomial. In particular, this is the first result on the local behavior of CD kernels for an almost periodic operator with singular spectrum.

As a complementary result, we describe the asymptotics of the scaling function, and the Christoffel function, at a fixed point of a quadratic iteration. This is the first such analysis for an almost periodic model with singular spectrum. It allows us to conclude that, at the fixed point, the local scaling of zeros of the polynomial of degreennis precisely of ordern−1/αn^{-1/\alpha}, whereα\alphais the local dimension of the measure. We also study the limit chain in this case, and prove that it can be parametrized by its asymptotics with respect to a Martin function (so-calledMM-type); this is the first result of this kind for a chain with a singular measure.

## Key words and phrases:universality limit, orthogonal polynomial, canonical system, quadratic iteration, Christoffel function

## 1.Introduction

Letμ\mube a probability measure onℝ{\mathbb{R}}which is not supported on finitely many points, and has finite moments:∫|x|n​𝑑μ​(x)<∞,∀n∈ℕ0.\displaystyle\int\lvert x\rvert^{n}d\mu(x)<\infty,\qquad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}.(1.1)

By applying the Gram–Schmidt process to the sequence of monomials(zk)k=0∞(z^{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}inL2​(ℝ,d​μ)L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},d\mu), one obtains the sequence of orthogonal polynomials(pk​(z))k=0∞(p_{k}(z))_{k=0}^{\infty}. The polynomialpkp_{k}haskkreal simple zeros, and the global and local asymptotics of the zeros are questions of classical interest[43,71,73,76,77]. The local behavior is studied using the Christoffel–Darboux (CD) kernelsK​(n,z,w)=∑k=0n−1pk​(z)​pk​(w)¯.K(n,z,w)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}p_{k}(z)\overline{p_{k}(w)}.

There is a long history of studying their scaling behavior around a pointξ∈ℝ\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}in regimes where a scaling limit exists, i.e.limn→∞K​(n,ξ+zr​(n),ξ+wr​(n))K​(n,ξ,ξ)=K∞​(z,w)\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{K\left(n,\xi+\frac{z}{r(n)},\xi+\frac{w}{r(n)}\right)}{K\left(n,\xi,\xi\right)}=K_{\infty}(z,w)(1.2)

for some scaling sequencer​(n)→∞r(n)\to\inftyand nonconstant limit kernelK∞K_{\infty}. The most studied phenomenon is so-called bulk universality, which corresponds to the sine kernel limitK∞​(z,w)=sin⁡(π​(z−w¯))/(π​(z−w¯))K_{\infty}(z,w)=\sin(\pi(z-\overline{w}))/(\pi(z-\overline{w})); cf.[5,17,18,19,28,29,30,31,36,37,41,57,58,59,61,63,72,78,80]for a very partial list of references. In particular, for measures corresponding to a determinate moment problem, bulk universality at the scaler​(n)=c​K​(n,ξ,ξ)r(n)=cK(n,\xi,\xi)for somec∈(0,∞)c\in(0,\infty), together with the conditionlimn→∞K​(n+1,ξ,ξ)K​(n,ξ,ξ)=1,\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{K(n+1,\xi,\xi)}{K(n,\xi,\xi)}=1,(1.3)

is equivalent toμ​([ξ,ξ±ϵ))/ϵ→c\mu([\xi,\xi\pm\epsilon))/\epsilon\to casϵ↓0\epsilon\downarrow 0[37]. Other limit kernels are also characterized in terms of local behaviors of the measureμ\muatξ\xi, and these are viewed as different universality classes, see e.g.[24,37,42,51,52]. For instance, Bessel kernels correspond to hard edge universality. In the non-Hermitian setting, other kernels have been studied for instance in[2,21,47].

Known universality classes arose from the study of measures on intervals (although they are now known to hold under local conditions which don’t require intervals in the support[36,37]), and they are not well suited to phenomena common in spectral theory, such as self-similarity and Cantor spectra[23].

The first goal of this paper is to describe new scaling behaviors associated with self-similar structures. Instead of a scaling limit, there will be a family of kernelsKs​(z,w)K_{s}(z,w)continuous ins∈(0,∞)s\in(0,\infty)with respect to locally uniform convergence, withKs​ρ=KsK_{s\rho}=K_{s}for someρ>1\rho>1, an increasing sequence ofr​(n)∈(0,∞)r(n)\in(0,\infty), andβ∈(−1,1)\beta\in(-1,1)such that1r​(n)1+β​K​(n,ξ+zr​(n),ξ+wr​(n))−Kr​(n)​(z,w)→0,n→∞.\frac{1}{r(n)^{1+\beta}}K\left(n,\xi+\frac{z}{r(n)},\xi+\frac{w}{r(n)}\right)-K_{r(n)}(z,w)\to 0,\qquad n\to\infty.(1.4)

This is a common way to describe the asymptotics of an object which doesn’t converge but asymptotically approaches periodic or almost periodic behavior, compare[84, Theorem 12.3]. We will provide a sufficient condition for (1.4) in terms of Weyl functionsmμ​(z)=∫1x−z​𝑑μ​(x),z∈ℂ+m_{\mu}(z)=\int\frac{1}{x-z}\,d\mu(x),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}(1.5)

with the notationℂ±={z∈ℂ∣±Im​z>0}.{\mathbb{C}}_{\pm}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid\pm\text{\rm Im\ }z>0\}.

We describe (1.4) as limit cycle behavior, due to the multiplicatively periodic conditionKs​ρ=KsK_{s\rho}=K_{s}, and view it as a new type of universality class. Even individually, the kernelsKsK_{s}have not previously appeared in the literature. We also note that the kernelsKsK_{s}for differents∈[1,ρ)s\in[1,\rho)are not merely rescalings of each other. Whereas previously studied limit kernels are expressed in terms of Bessel or hypergeometric functions, the limit cycle corresponds to a self-similar canonical system. The solutions of this canonical system are the special functions associated with the universality class.

To formulate corollaries about local zero spacing, we will label the zeros ofpnp_{n}relative toξ\xibyξk(n)\xi_{k}^{(n)}, so that⋯<ξ−1(n)<ξ0(n)≤ξ<ξ1(n)<ξ2(n)<…\dots<\xi_{-1}^{(n)}<\xi_{0}^{(n)}\leq\xi<\xi_{1}^{(n)}<\xi_{2}^{(n)}<\dots

Of course, for eachnn, only finitely many of these are well-defined. When we write a limit statement involving someξk(n)\xi_{k}^{(n)}, it is implied as part of that statement thatξk(n)\xi_{k}^{(n)}exists for all large enoughnn.
We will use the notationf≲gf\lesssim giflim supf/g≤C\limsup f/g\leq Cfor someC<∞C<\inftyandf≍gf\asymp giff≲gf\lesssim gandg≲fg\lesssim f. Typical of our results will be that fork<lk<l,ξl(n)−ξk(n)≍K​(n,ξ,ξ)−1/(1+β),n→∞.\xi_{l}^{(n)}-\xi_{k}^{(n)}\asymp K(n,\xi,\xi)^{-1/(1+\beta)},\qquad n\to\infty.(1.6)

The fine structure of zeros for singular measures is a well-known open problem, popularized by Simon in[81, Problem 1.4]. The only general result is a lower bound in terms of transfer matrices[54, Theorems 2.1, 2.2]; applied to our setting, this would giveξl(n)−ξk(n)≳K​(n,ξ,ξ)−(1+|β|)/(1+β),n→∞,\xi_{l}^{(n)}-\xi_{k}^{(n)}\gtrsim K(n,\xi,\xi)^{-(1+\lvert\beta\rvert)/(1+\beta)},\qquad n\to\infty,

so (1.6) improves the exponent and provides a matching upper bound.

The second goal is to show that this behavior occurs in two prominent examples. The first is the middle third Cantor measure, whose orthogonal polynomials have been studied as a natural model for the zero spacing for singular measures[50]. The second are balanced/equilibrium measures of real Julia sets of expanding polynomials; these measures are the canonical model in inverse spectral theory of almost periodic Jacobi operators with singular continuous spectrum[7,8,9,10,64,75].

In spectral theory, absolutely continuous spectrum is often encountered on intervals, whereas singular spectrum is often encountered on Cantor sets, but there are many counterexamples to this. Accordingly, there is no formal connection between the spectral type and the universality class, and we will provide an example of limit cycle behavior in an a.c. measure. Still, whereas bulk universality holds a.e. on the a.c. part of the measure[5,36], singular measures are harder to study. Our application to balanced measures of Julia sets is the first result on scaling behavior of the CD kernels for almost periodic operators with singular spectrum.

In our examples, the behavior (1.4) holds with a sequence such thatr​(n)→∞r(n)\to\inftyandr​(n+1)/r​(n)→1r(n+1)/r(n)\to 1asn→∞n\to\infty. This ensures that every kernel in the limit cycle is indeed an accumulation point of the rescaled CD kernels.

The third goal of our paper is to study the growth rate of the scaling functionr​(n)r(n), which is tightly related to the sequenceK​(n,ξ,ξ)K(n,\xi,\xi): it follows already from (1.4) evaluated atz=w=0z=w=0thatK​(n,ξ,ξ)≍r​(n)1+β.K(n,\xi,\xi)\asymp r(n)^{1+\beta}.

The reciprocal ofK​(n,ξ,ξ)K(n,\xi,\xi)is known as the Christoffel function. Its asymptotics are a question of classical interest, traditionally studied on the absolutely continuous spectrum[15,33,45,62,79]. For Stahl–Totik regular measures, under a Lebesgue point assumption for the density and a local Szegő condition,K​(n,ξ,ξ)∼nK(n,\xi,\xi)\sim nasn→∞n\to\infty[62,79]. Whereas the scaling behavior of CD kernels depends on the local behavior of the measure, the asymptotic behavior ofK​(n,ξ,ξ)K(n,\xi,\xi)depends also on the global properties of the measureμ\mu; see e.g. the discussion preceding Theorem 1.2 in[79]for the role of Stahl–Totik regularity. The asymptotics are also known to be different for measures of unbounded support[31].

Singular measures have much more varied behavior, and an analysis of Christoffel function is expected to be highly model-dependent. We develop this analysis precisely for balanced measures on Julia sets of quadratic polynomials, at a fixed point of the quadratic iteration. We prove thatK​(n,ξ,ξ)≍n,n→∞.K(n,\xi,\xi)\asymp n,\qquad n\to\infty.

In fact, we will describe the oscillations inK​(n,ξ,ξ)/nK(n,\xi,\xi)/nand show thatK​(n,ξ,ξ)∼n​b​(n),n→∞\displaystyle K(n,\xi,\xi)\sim nb(n),\qquad n\to\infty(1.7)r​(n)∼nκ​c​(n),n→∞\displaystyle r(n)\sim n^{\kappa}c(n),\qquad n\to\infty(1.8)

for some explicit continuous functionsb,c:(0,∞)→(0,∞)b,c:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)which are multiplicatively periodic in the sense thatb​(2​ℓ)=b​(ℓ)b(2\ell)=b(\ell)andc​(2​ℓ)=c​(ℓ)c(2\ell)=c(\ell).

In fact, the main aspect of our analysis is the study of the limit cycle for this particular universality class. The kernels in the limit cycle are obtained from a chain ofJJ-inner functions, and we will prove that this chain can be parametrized by its growth rate relative to the Martin function (known asMM-type); this is the first positive result about parametrization byMM-type for a chain with a singular measure. From the chain parametrized in this way, the functionsb​(n)b(n),c​(n)c(n)and the asymptotics (1.7), (1.8) are obtained.

Quadratic iteration has a central place in dynamics, and it is our intention that this analysis helps in understanding more general dynamically defined settings.

In the remainder of this introduction, we will formulate the results precisely and provide some further context.

## 1.1.Limit cycle behavior and local zero spacing

We will describe asymptotic behavior for2×22\times 2matrix kernels, from which (1.4) will follow. To formulate this, we need the following definitions. Orthonormal polynomials of the second kindqjq_{j}are defined byqj​(z)=∫pj​(x)−pj​(z)x−z​𝑑μ​(x),j≥0.q_{j}(z)=\int\frac{p_{j}(x)-p_{j}(z)}{x-z}d\mu(x),\qquad j\geq 0.

We will study the2×22\times 2matrix kernels𝒦μ​(n,z,w)=(∑k=0n−1qk​(z)​qk​(w)¯∑k=0n−1qk​(z)​pk​(w)¯∑k=0n−1pk​(z)​qk​(w)¯∑k=0n−1pk​(z)​pk​(w)¯),\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{\mu}(n,z,w)=\begin{pmatrix}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}q_{k}(z)\overline{q_{k}(w)}&\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}q_{k}(z)\overline{p_{k}(w)}\\
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}p_{k}(z)\overline{q_{k}(w)}&\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}p_{k}(z)\overline{p_{k}(w)}\end{pmatrix},(1.9)

which encode information about all eigensolutions of the associated Jacobi matrix, and contain the CD kernel as a diagonal entry. Scaling limits of these kernels are not kernels generated by orthogonal polynomials. They typically correspond, in a more abstract way, to a measure without finite moments. To formulate our results properly and describe the limit cycle, we must introduce a larger space of2×22\times 2matrix kernels and recall elements ofJJ-multiplicative function theory[4,27,68,70].

All prior studies of the scaling behavior of CD kernels described scaling limits. In our result, we derive limit cycle behavior of the CD kernels from oscillatory boundary behavior of the Weyl function.

## Theorem 1.2.

Letμ\mube a measure onℝ{\mathbb{R}}corresponding to a determinate moment problem,mμm_{\mu}the associated Weyl function and𝒦μ​(L,⋅,⋅){\mathcal{K}}_{\mu}(L,\cdot,\cdot)the associated kernels (1.9), (1.12). Fixξ∈ℝ\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}and assume that there existβ∈(−1,1)\beta\in(-1,1),ρ>1\rho>1, and a functionω:ℂ+→ℂ∖{0}\omega:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}which satisfiesω​(ρ​z)=ω​(z),∀z∈ℂ+,\omega(\rho z)=\omega(z),\qquad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+},(1.16)

such that the Weyl function has the normal boundary behaviorlimy→0|e−β​i​π/2​y−β​mμ​(ξ+i​y)−ω​(i​y)|=0.\displaystyle\lim_{y\to 0}|e^{-\beta i\pi/2}y^{-\beta}m_{\mu}(\xi+iy)-\omega(iy)|=0.(1.17)

ForL>0L>0definer​(L)=r​(L,ξ):=(𝒦μ)11​(L,ξ,ξ)​(𝒦μ)22​(L,ξ,ξ).\displaystyle r(L)=r(L,\xi):=\sqrt{({\mathcal{K}}_{\mu})_{11}(L,\xi,\xi)({\mathcal{K}}_{\mu})_{22}(L,\xi,\xi)}.(1.18)

Then, there exists a family of matrix kernelsℒs∈ℂ​𝔻​(2){\mathcal{L}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{D}}(2),s∈(0,∞)s\in(0,\infty), and a constantρ>1\rho>1, such thatℒs​ρ=ℒs{\mathcal{L}}_{s\rho}={\mathcal{L}}_{s}for allssandlimL→∞(1r​(L)​𝒱​(r​(L)β)​𝒦μ​(L,ξ+zr​(L),ξ+wr​(L))​𝒱​(r​(L)β)∗−ℒr​(L)​(z,w))=0\displaystyle\lim\limits_{L\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{r(L)}{\mathcal{V}}(r(L)^{\beta}){\mathcal{K}}_{\mu}\left(L,\xi+\frac{z}{r(L)},\xi+\frac{w}{r(L)}\right){\mathcal{V}}(r(L)^{\beta})^{*}-{\mathcal{L}}_{r(L)}(z,w)\right)=0(1.19)

uniformly for(z,w)(z,w)in compact subsets ofℂ×ℂ{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{C}}. In particular, (1.4) follows from (1.19) by taking a diagonal entry.

With the branch ofzβz^{\beta}onℂ+{\mathbb{C}}_{+}with0<arg⁡(zβ)<β​π0<\arg(z^{\beta})<\beta\pi, the functionmβ,ω​(z)=zβ​ω​(z)m_{\beta,\omega}(z)=z^{\beta}\omega(z)is a Herglotz function. This function uniquely determines the limit cycle of kernels:

then the limit cycle consists of the kernelsℒs​(z,w)=1s​𝒱​(sβ)​𝒦​(t​(s),zs,ws)​𝒱​(sβ)∗.{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(z,w)=\frac{1}{s}{\mathcal{V}}(s^{\beta}){\mathcal{K}}\left(t(s),\frac{z}{s},\frac{w}{s}\right){\mathcal{V}}(s^{\beta})^{*}.(1.21)

Theorem1.2is only a special case of our more general Theorem3.6. Theorem3.6is formulated for canonical systems, and Theorem1.2is its application to orthogonal polynomials. Theorem3.6has analogous applications to continuum Schrödinger operators, Dirac systems, Krein strings, and orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle.

We note that the geometric mean of diagonal entries (1.18), used here as a scaling function, was previously used in[48]in the setting of Jacobi matrices and more generally for canonical systems in[53]in order to estimate the functiony↦|mμ​(ξ+i​y)|y\mapsto|m_{\mu}(\xi+iy)|.

By Theorem1.2, the functionmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}determines the universality class. Moreover, transformations of the formm~β,ω​(z)={c1​c2​mβ,ω​(c2​z)c1>0,c2>0c1​c2​mβ,ω​(c2​z¯)¯c1>0,c2<0\tilde{m}_{\beta,\omega}(z)=\begin{cases}c_{1}c_{2}m_{\beta,\omega}(c_{2}z)&c_{1}>0,c_{2}>0\\
c_{1}c_{2}\overline{m_{\beta,\omega}(c_{2}\overline{z})}&c_{1}>0,c_{2}<0\end{cases}(1.22)

correspond to simple transformations of the spectral measure: scalar multiplication byc1c_{1}and pushforward by the linear mapλ↦c2​λ\lambda\mapsto c_{2}\lambda. These correspond to equally simple transformations of the kernels in the limit cycle. Thus, ifmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}andm~β,ω\tilde{m}_{\beta,\omega}are related by (1.22), we will say that they correspond to the same universality class.

The limit cycle corresponds to a canonical system, which we think of as the limiting object representing the local behavior atξ\xi. This canonical system has its own measure and spectral type, not to be confused with the orthogonality measure and its spectral type. Previously studied universality classes, where there is convergence to a single limit kernel, correspond toω​(z)≡ω0\omega(z)\equiv\omega_{0}for some constantω0\omega_{0}, so the limiting object had an a.c. measure. The multiplicatively periodic functionω\omegaallows for much more freedom. We will provide examples where the measure ofmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}is purely singular continuous. This is, up to our knowledge, the first example of universality classes where the limiting kernels correspond to a singular measure.

We will now formulate corollaries for zero spacing.
We will say that the universality class is an edge universality class ifmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}has an analytic extension toℂ∖(−∞,0]{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(-\infty,0]or toℂ∖[0,∞){\mathbb{C}}\setminus[0,\infty)which satisfiesmβ,ω​(z¯)¯=mβ,ω​(z)\overline{m_{\beta,\omega}(\overline{z})}=m_{\beta,\omega}(z)(for constantω\omega, this corresponds to the well known hard edge universality classes). For zero spacing, edge universality classes are qualitatively different: the nearby zeros of orthogonal polynomials will be on one side of the pointξ\xi, with at most one exception. We will also state a result about the distance from the smallest zero to the minimum of the spectrum.

## Corollary 1.3.

Under the assumptions of Theorem1.2, letξ\xibe such that the Weyl function has the normal boundary behavior (1.17). Then:
- (a)

If the universality class ofmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}is not an edge universality class, then for anyk,l∈ℤk,l\in{\mathbb{Z}}withk<lk<l, (1.6) holds.
- (b)

If the universality class ofmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}is an edge universality class, without loss of generality saymβ,ω​(z¯)¯=mβ,ω​(z)\overline{m_{\beta,\omega}(\overline{z})}=m_{\beta,\omega}(z)onℂ∖[0,∞){\mathbb{C}}\setminus[0,\infty), then for anyk,l∈ℕk,l\in{\mathbb{N}}, withk<lk<l, (1.6) holds. Moreover, for anyR>0R>0, for all large enoughnn,pnp_{n}can have at most one zero in[ξ−R​K​(n,ξ,ξ)−1/(1+β),ξ][\xi-RK(n,\xi,\xi)^{-1/(1+\beta)},\xi], and along a subsequence where such a zeroξ0(nj)\xi_{0}^{(n_{j})}exists, (1.6) holds also for0=k<l0=k<l.
- (c)

Ifsupp⁡μ\operatorname{supp}\muis bounded below, and at the pointξ=min⁡supp⁡μ\xi=\min\operatorname{supp}\mu, the Weyl function satisfies the normal boundary behavior (1.17), then for1≤l1\leq l,ξl(n)−ξ≍K​(n,ξ,ξ)−1/(1+β)\xi_{l}^{(n)}-\xi\asymp K(n,\xi,\xi)^{-1/(1+\beta)}(1.23)

The constants implicit in (1.6) and (1.23) depend only onβ,ω,k,l\beta,\omega,k,l.

## 1.2.The Cantor measure

We now recall the middle third Cantor setC⊂[0,1]C\subset[0,1], which can be written in terms ofT​(x)=3​|x−12|−12T(x)=3\lvert x-\frac{1}{2}\rvert-\frac{1}{2}asC=∩n=0∞(T−1)n​([0,1])C=\cap_{n=0}^{\infty}(T^{-1})^{n}([0,1]), and the middle third Cantor measure.

## Theorem 1.4.

Letμ\mube the middle third Cantor measure. For anyξ∈ℚ∩supp⁡μ\xi\in{\mathbb{Q}}\cap\operatorname{supp}\mu, the conclusions of Theorem1.2hold atξ\xi, withβ=log⁡2log⁡3−1.\beta=\frac{\log 2}{\log 3}-1.(1.24)

Moreover, ifξ,η∈ℚ∩supp⁡μ\xi,\eta\in{\mathbb{Q}}\cap\operatorname{supp}\muare such that for somem,n∈ℕm,n\in{\mathbb{N}},3m​ξ−⌊3m​ξ⌋=3n​η−⌊3n​η⌋3^{m}\xi-\lfloor 3^{m}\xi\rfloor=3^{n}\eta-\lfloor 3^{n}\eta\rfloor, then they are in the same universality class.

In particular, all gap edges are in the same edge universality class as the endpoint0. The relevance of rationality in this result is that rational points are eventually periodic in the dynamics on the Cantor set, and the relevance of the condition onξ,η\xi,\etais that they are eventually on the same periodic orbit. For the middle third Cantor measure, a lower bound on the zero spacing was proved in[50].

In the above example,β+1\beta+1is the Hausdorff dimension, but we wish to reiterate that spectral type is not directly related to the local scaling behavior of the kernel. Bulk universality was originally studied for a.c. measures but can also occur for singular measures[17]and even for pure point measures[37]. Likewise, we now point out an example of limit cycle behavior withβ<0\beta<0in an absolutely continuous measure.

## Example 1.5.

For the absolutely continuous measured​μ​(x)=∑n=0∞(32)n+1​χ(2/3n+1,1/3n)​(x)​d​x,d\mu(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{n+1}\chi_{(2/3^{n+1},1/3^{n})}(x)\,dx,

the CD kernels have limit cycle behavior atξ=0\xi=0, as in the conclusions of Theorem1.2, withβ\betaagain given by (1.24). At every nonzeroξ∈supp⁡μ\xi\in\operatorname{supp}\mu, the CD kernels obey bulk universality or hard edge universality.

## 1.3.Balanced/equilibrium measures on Julia sets

LetTTbe a polynomial with a real Julia set𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0}. In particular,TThas real coefficients.
Itsnn-th iterate is denotedT∘nT^{\circ n}. The polynomial is said to be expanding on its Julia set if there exista>0a>0,λ>1\lambda>1such that|(T∘n)′​(z)|≥a​λn\lvert(T^{\circ n})^{\prime}(z)\rvert\geq a\lambda^{n}for allz∈𝖤0z\in{\mathsf{E}}_{0}andn∈ℕn\in{\mathbb{N}}[40, Section 3]. The Julia set is then of the form𝖤0:=ℂ∖{z∈ℂ∣T∘n​(z)→∞,n→∞}.\displaystyle{\mathsf{E}}_{0}:={\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid T^{\circ n}(z)\to\infty,\ n\to\infty\}.(1.25)

Letμ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}denote the balanced measure of𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0}, i.e., the unique probability measure such that for everyf∈C​(𝖤0)f\in C({\mathsf{E}}_{0})∫1d​∑T​(y)=xf​(y)​d​μ𝖤0​(x)=∫f​(x)​𝑑μ𝖤0​(x).\int\frac{1}{d}\sum_{T(y)=x}f(y)d\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}(x)=\int f(x)d\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}(x).

From the perspective of potential theory,μ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}is the equilibrium measure of𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0}[20], and the harmonic measure for the point∞\infty. There are no prior results about rescaling limits of CD-kernels forμ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}. We show that these balanced measures provide a natural application of Theorem1.2; in fact, they were the motivating example for this work.

## Theorem 1.6.

LetTTbe an expanding polynomial,d=deg⁡T>1d=\deg T>1, with a real Julia set𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0}. Letμ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}denote the balanced measure of𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0}. Letξ∈𝖤0\xi\in{\mathsf{E}}_{0}be an eventually fixed or periodic point, i.e., there exists a fixed or periodic pointη\etasuch thatT∘n​(ξ)=ηT^{\circ n}(\xi)=\etafor somenn. Then the matrix kernels associated toμ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}obey the limit cycle behavior (1.19) at the pointξ\xi. The constantβ\betais given byβ=q​log⁡dlog⁡|(T∘q)′​(η)|−1\beta=\frac{q\log d}{\log\lvert(T^{\circ q})^{\prime}(\eta)\rvert}-1

whereqqis the period ofη\eta. The limit cycle, up to rescaling, depends only on the eventual orbit of the point.

From the invariance ofμ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}it follows that it is singular continuous[9]. In many cases,μ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}is known to correspond to an almost periodic (in fact, limit periodic) Jacobi matrix[10]; this is expected to hold in the general case, but it is still an open problem. In particular, Theorem1.6is the first result to describe scaling behavior of CD kernels for some almost periodic Jacobi matrices with singular spectrum.

We regard it as an interesting open problem whether this limit cycle behavior holds in other almost periodic (and related) models of interest.

## 1.4.Asymptotics of the scaling function and parametrization byMM-type

We now turn to two highly model-dependent questions: the asymptotic behavior ofK​(n,ξ,ξ)K(n,\xi,\xi)asn→∞n\to\infty, and the nature of the chain corresponding to the limit cycle. We will answer these questions in the canonical model of interest: that of a quadratic polynomial with a real Julia set, at a fixed point.

We write the polynomial in the formT​(z)=z​(z+ρ)T(z)=z(z+\rho)

with−ρ>2-\rho>2(by conjugating by the dynamics, this is equivalent to the often used formT​(w)=w2−λT(w)=w^{2}-\lambda, withλ>2\lambda>2).
We will study the fixed point0, which is the internal fixed point. As before,𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0}denotes its Julia set andμ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}the balanced measure. SinceTTis expanding and0is a fixed point ofTT, by Theorem1.6,𝒦μ𝖤0{\mathcal{K}}_{\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}}has a limit cycle atξ=0\xi=0.

To describe the chain corresponding to the limit cycle in this case, we have to recall exponential type andMM-type and their importance in spectral theory.

## Remark 1.7.
- (i)

Recall that the order of an entire functionffisρf:=lim supr→∞ln⁡ln⁡max|z|≤r⁡|f​(z)|ln⁡r\displaystyle\rho_{f}:=\limsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{\ln\ln\max_{|z|\leq r}|f(z)|}{\ln r}

and the type with respect to this order isτf:=lim supr→∞ln⁡max|z|≤r⁡|f​(z)|rρf.\tau_{f}:=\limsup_{r\to\infty}\frac{\ln\max_{|z|\leq r}|f(z)|}{r^{\rho_{f}}}.
- (ii)

In direct spectral theory, transfer matrices obey some universal asymptotics at∞\infty. For instance, thenn-step transfer matrix of the Jacobi recursion is a polynomial of degreenn, Dirac transfer matrices over an interval of lengthℓ\ellhave order11and exponential typeℓ\ell, and Schrödinger transfer matrices over an interval of lengthℓ\ellhave order1/21/2and typeℓ\ell[67,60]. These asymptotics underlie important results such as proofs of the Borg–Marchenko theorem or Stahl–Totik regularity theory (compare[12,71,34,35]). Note that the length of the interval is encoded as the type; in other words, the natural parameter in direct spectral theory is encoded in the asymptotics of the transfer matrix.
- (iii)

InJJ-theory, there are no universal asymptotics, and asymptotics at∞\inftyare not preserved by locally uniform convergence. Our transfer matrices have exponential type given by the Krein–de Branges formula[49],[27, Section 39],[16, Theorem 1.11],[69], but the exponential type is often0and doesn’t serve as a parameter for the family of transfer matrices.
- (iv)

Now we provide a precise definition of theMM-type. If𝖤⊂ℝ{\mathsf{E}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}is closed and unbounded,∞\inftyis a boundary point of the Denjoy domainΩ=ℂ∖𝖤\Omega={\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathsf{E}}. Assume in addition thatΩ\Omegais Greenian. Then aMartin functionat∞\inftyis a positive harmonic function inΩ\Omega, which is bounded on bounded subsets ofΩ\Omegaand vanishes continuously at every Dirichlet boundary point ofEE. It is called symmetric if in additionM​(z¯)=M​(z)M(\overline{z})=M(z). A symmetric Martin function is unique up to normalization[3,11]. We say that an entire function,ff, hasMM-typeℓ>0\ell>0, if it is of bounded characteristic inℂ∖𝖤{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathsf{E}}[83, p. 861]andℓ=lim supy→∞log⁡|f​(i​y)|M​(i​y).\ell=\limsup\limits_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log|f(iy)|}{M(iy)}.

Using the matrix norm, theMM-type of a matrix function is defined analogously, and this is a natural way to quantify the growth of transfer matrices.
- (v)

For a periodic canonical system, Floquet theory describes its spectrum𝖤{\mathsf{E}}in terms of the monodromy matrix. Typically,𝖤{\mathsf{E}}is unbounded, i.e., doesn’t correspond to orthogonal polynomials, and we can consider a symmetric Martin functionMM. If the spectrum satisfies some known regularity properties (the Widom condition and DCT property), it was proved in[85]that the chain is parametrized in terms ofMM-type, i.e., each transfer matrix has finiteMM-type and for eachℓ>0\ell>0there is a unique transfer matrix in the chain which hasMM-type. In[14], the same was proved for almost periodic canonical systems with the Widom and DCT properties. In these classes, spectra are of positive Lebesgue measure and spectral measures are absolutely continuous.
- (vi)

Parametrization byMM-type is not always possible. For instance, the transfer matrix for the free Schrödinger operator is𝔄S​(t,z)=(cos⁡(z​t)sin⁡(z​t)z−z​sin⁡(z​t)cos⁡(z​t)){\mathfrak{A}}_{S}(t,z)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\sqrt{z}t)&\frac{\sin(\sqrt{z}t)}{\sqrt{z}}\\
-\sqrt{z}\sin(\sqrt{z}t)&\cos(\sqrt{z}t)\end{pmatrix}

and the transfer matrix for the free Dirac operator is𝔄D​(t,z)=(cos⁡(z​t)sin⁡(z​t)−sin⁡(z​t)cos⁡(z​t)).{\mathfrak{A}}_{D}(t,z)=\left(\begin{matrix}\cos(zt)&\sin(zt)\\
-\sin(zt)&\cos(zt)\end{matrix}\right).

Building a chimera of these two families gives aJJ-monotonic family𝔄​(t,z)={𝔄D​(t,z),t≤1𝔄D​(1,z)​𝔄S​(t−1,z),t≥1{\mathfrak{A}}(t,z)=\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{A}}_{D}(t,z),&\quad t\leq 1\\
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt{\mathfrak{A}}_{D}(1,z){\mathfrak{A}}_{S}(t-1,z),&\quad t\geq 1\end{cases}(1.26)

In this case for arbitraryt>0t>0, the entries of𝔄​(t,z){\mathfrak{A}}(t,z)are of order11, but fort≥1t\geq 1, the type remains constant. Hence, a parametrization of𝔄{\mathfrak{A}}in terms ofMM-type is not possible in such an example.
- (vii)

Chimeras are common objects inJJ-theory. As an example consider a classical Krein problem on extensions of Hermitian-positive functionsS​(t)S(t)from a finite interval(−ℓ,ℓ)(-\ell,\ell)to the whole axis[1, Chapt. 5, §3]. Each extension can be associated with a measureσ\sigmaon the real axis, which provides its Bochner representation, and the measure in turn corresponds to a Herglotz function. In the case of non-uniqueness, the collection of Herglotz functions is parametrised in terms of a fractional linear transform with entireJJ-inner function𝔄​(ℓ,z){\mathfrak{A}}(\ell,z)as the matrix of this transform. This matrix is of exponential order one and its type depends onℓ\ell. However, as soon asS​(t)S(t)possesses an additional smoothness in the origin (differentiable or just continuous)𝔄​(ℓ,z){\mathfrak{A}}(\ell,z)contains with necessity a polynomial multiplier in its multiplicative representation, i.e., the factor corresponding to a moment problem. Indeed, each derivative of the Hermitian-positive function requires existence of an additional moment for the representative measureσ\sigma.
- (viii)

Chimeras have often led to counterexamples of conjectures in spectral theory. In[86], they were used to prove that Widom DCT sets are not necessarily homogeneous, and that weakly homogeneous sets are not necessarily DCT. In[82], they were used to disprove the Kotani–Last conjecture.

The main theorem of this part of the paper is that the chain of entireJJ-inner matrix functions associated to the limit cycle of𝒦μ𝖤0{\mathcal{K}}_{\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}}can be parametrized by theMM-type (i.e., in this sense this chain is not a chimera). In order to formulate the precise statement, we will now define the Herglotz function corresponding to the limit cycle and theMM-type.

LetT​(z)=z​(z+ρ)T(z)=z(z+\rho)andμ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}be the balanced measure of its Julia set. Definem𝖤0​(z)=∫d​μ𝖤0​(x)x−z.m_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}(z)=\int\frac{d\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}(x)}{x-z}.(1.27)

The Herglotz function corresponding to the limit cycle will be given in terms of a well-studied function in iteration theory. It is a classical result that the following limit exists, see e.g.[44, Sect. 3]F​(z)=limn→∞Fn​(z),Fn​(z)=T∘n​(z/ρn).F(z)=\lim_{n\to\infty}F_{n}(z),\quad F_{n}(z)=T^{\circ n}(z/\rho^{n}).

The limit functionF​(z)F(z)satisfies the Poincaré equationT​(F​(z))=F​(ρ​z),F​(0)=0,F′​(0)=1,T(F(z))=F(\rho z),\quad F(0)=0,F^{\prime}(0)=1,(1.28)

and we thus refer to it as the Poincaré function. Set𝖤:=F−1​(𝖤0){\mathsf{E}}:=F^{-1}({\mathsf{E}}_{0})and define the functionm𝖤​(z)=F′​(z)​m𝖤0​(F​(z)).m_{\mathsf{E}}(z)=F^{\prime}(z)m_{{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}}(F(z)).

The functionm𝖤​(z)m_{\mathsf{E}}(z)is again a Herglotz function and admits an integral representation similar to (1.27)m𝖤​(z)=∫d​μ𝖤​(x)x−z.m_{{\mathsf{E}}}(z)=\int\frac{d\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}}(x)}{x-z}.

The measureμ𝖤\mu_{\mathsf{E}}is supported on𝖤{\mathsf{E}}and therefore in particular is again singular. Note that in contrast to𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0},𝖤{\mathsf{E}}is an unbounded set and thus∞\inftyis a boundary point ofΩ=ℂ∖𝖤\Omega={\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathsf{E}}.

## Theorem 1.8.

Let𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0}be the Julia set ofT​(z)=z​(z+ρ)T(z)=z(z+\rho), forρ<1−13\rho<1-\sqrt{13}. Let𝖤=F−1​(𝖤0){\mathsf{E}}=F^{-1}({\mathsf{E}}_{0})andm𝖤​(z)=F′​(z)​m𝖤0​(F​(z))m_{\mathsf{E}}(z)=F^{\prime}(z)m_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}(F(z)). Let𝔇​(t,z){\mathfrak{D}}(t,z)be the de Branges’ trace parametrization of𝒞​(m𝖤){\mathscr{C}}(m_{\mathsf{E}}). Then theMM-type of𝔇​(t,z){\mathfrak{D}}(t,z)exists for everyt>0t>0, i.e.,ℓ​(t)=limy→∞log⁡‖𝔇​(t,i​y)‖M​(i​y).\ell(t)=\lim\limits_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log\|{\mathfrak{D}}(t,iy)\|}{M(iy)}.

Moreover,ℓ​(t)\ell(t)is continuous and bijective. Hence, denoting byt​(ℓ)t(\ell)its inverse,𝔇​(t​(ℓ),z){\mathfrak{D}}(t(\ell),z)provides a parametrization of𝒞​(m𝖤){\mathscr{C}}(m_{\mathsf{E}})in terms of theMM-type.

## Remark 1.9.
- (i)

Theorem1.8is the first example where a parametrization in terms of theMM-type was proven for a system with a singular spectral measure.
- (ii)

Let us explain the condition onρ\rho. The proof of Theorem1.8relies on limit periodicity of the Jacobi matrix associated with the balanced measure of the Julia set of the polynomialT​(z)=z​(z+ρ)T(z)=z(z+\rho). This fact is known for the Jacobi matrix associated with the balanced measure of the Julia set of the quadratic polynomialT~​(z)=z2−λ\tilde{T}(z)=z^{2}-\lambda, forλ>3\lambda>3,[9,13,75]and was improved in[6]toλ>2.192\lambda>2.192. The corresponding claim for the Jacobi matrix associated withTTfollows, since the dynamical systems generated byTTandT~\tilde{T}are equivalent withλ=ρ2/4−ρ/2\lambda=\rho^{2}/4-\rho/2. Thus, our condition onρ\rhocorresponds toλ>3\lambda>3.

Let𝒦𝔇​(t​(ℓ),z,w)=𝔇​(t​(ℓ),z)​J​𝔇​(t​(ℓ),w)∗−Jz−w¯.{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathfrak{D}}(t(\ell),z,w)=\frac{{\mathfrak{D}}(t(\ell),z)J{\mathfrak{D}}(t(\ell),w)^{*}-J}{z-\overline{w}}.

We now describe the asymptotics of the Christoffel function and the matrix scaling functionr​(n)r(n):

## Theorem 1.10.

Letμ𝖤0\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}be the balanced measure for the polynomialT​(z)=z​(z+ρ)T(z)=z(z+\rho), where−ρ>2-\rho>2and defineκ=log⁡|ρ|/log⁡2\kappa=\log|\rho|/\log 2.
- (a)K​(n,0,0)\displaystyle K(n,0,0)≍n,n→∞\displaystyle\asymp n,\qquad n\to\infty(1.29)r​(n)\displaystyle r(n)≍nκ,n→∞\displaystyle\asymp n^{\kappa},\qquad n\to\infty(1.30)
- (b)

Assume that−ρ>13−1-\rho>\sqrt{13}-1. Then the functionsb,c:(0,∞)→(0,∞)b,c:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)given byb​(ℓ)=1ℓ​(𝒦𝔇)22​(t​(ℓ),0,0)b(\ell)=\frac{1}{\ell}({\mathcal{K}}_{\mathfrak{D}})_{22}(t(\ell),0,0)(1.31)c​(ℓ)=1ℓκ​(𝒦𝔇)11​(t​(ℓ),0,0)​(𝒦𝔇)22​(t​(ℓ),0,0)c(\ell)=\frac{1}{\ell^{\kappa}}\sqrt{({\mathcal{K}}_{\mathfrak{D}})_{11}(t(\ell),0,0)({\mathcal{K}}_{\mathfrak{D}})_{22}(t(\ell),0,0)}(1.32)

are continuous, multiplicatively periodic in the sense thatb​(2​ℓ)=b​(ℓ),c​(2​ℓ)=c​(ℓ),b(2\ell)=b(\ell),\qquad c(2\ell)=c(\ell),

and (1.7), (1.8) hold.

For instance, under the assumptions of (b), (1.29) can be written in the more explicit formminℓ∈[1,2)⁡b≤lim infn→∞K​(n,ξ,ξ)n≤lim supn→∞K​(n,ξ,ξ)n≤maxℓ∈[1,2)⁡b.\min_{\ell\in[1,2)}b\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{K(n,\xi,\xi)}{n}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{K(n,\xi,\xi)}{n}\leq\max_{\ell\in[1,2)}b.

For dyadic rationalsℓ=k/2s\ell=k/2^{s}, we can give an explicit formula for𝔇​(t​(ℓ),z){\mathfrak{D}}(t(\ell),z), see Theorem5.7and (5.28).

In Section2, we will review some properties of canonical systems and their rescalings. In Section3we will study self-similar canonical systems and those with multiplicatively periodically modulated scaling behavior; in particular we will prove Theorem1.2, Corollary1.3, and Theorem1.4. In Section4we study balanced measures on Julia sets and prove Theorem1.6. In Section5we prove Theorem1.8and Theorem1.10.

## Acknowledgements

We are thankful to M. Sodin, who pointed more than 40 years ago to a possible use of the Poincaré function in studying canonical systems with singular spectrum.

## 2.Preliminaries

In this section we recall some facts and basic definitions about canonical systems, some associated objects and continuity properties among those objects.

## 2.1.Canonical systems, CD-kernels and Weyl functions

## Definition 2.1.

We call a functionH:[0,∞)→ℝ2×2H:[0,\infty)\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2\times 2}aHamiltonianifH∈Lloc1​([0,∞))H\in L^{1}_{\text{\rm loc}}([0,\infty)),H​(t)≥0H(t)\geq 0for a.e.t∈(0,∞)t\in(0,\infty), andH​(t)≠0H(t)\neq 0for a.e.t∈(0,∞)t\in(0,\infty). We assume in addition thatHHis in the limit point case at∞\infty, i.e.,∫0∞tr​H​(t)​𝑑t=∞.\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\text{\rm tr}H(t)dt=\infty.

We denote the set of all such Hamiltonians byℍ{\mathbb{H}}. We call a Hamiltoniantrace normalized, iftr​H​(t)=1\text{\rm tr}H(t)=1for a.e.t∈(0,∞)t\in(0,\infty). The set of all trace normalized canonical systems on[0,∞)[0,\infty)will be denoted byℍ^\hat{\mathbb{H}}.

IfH∈ℍH\in{\mathbb{H}}, the associatedhalf-linecanonical systemis the following differential expression∂t𝔄H​(t,z)​J=z​𝔄H​(t,z)​H​(t),𝔄H​(0,z)=(1001),\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\mathfrak{A}}_{H}(t,z)J=z{\mathfrak{A}}_{H}(t,z)H(t),\quad{\mathfrak{A}}_{H}(0,z)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},(2.1)

wheret∈[0,∞)t\in[0,\infty),JJis given by (1.10) andz∈ℂz\in{\mathbb{C}}is the spectral parameter. The matrix function𝔄H{\mathfrak{A}}_{H}is called thetransfer matrixof the system.

LetH1,H2∈ℍH_{1},H_{2}\in{\mathbb{H}}. We say thatH2H_{2}is areparametrizationofH1H_{1}and writeH2∼H1H_{2}\sim H_{1}, if there exists an increasing
bijectionγ:(0,∞)→(0,∞)\gamma:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)such thatγ\gammaandγ−1\gamma^{-1}are absolutely continuous andH2​(t)=H1​(γ​(t))​γ′​(t).H_{2}(t)=H_{1}(\gamma(t))\gamma^{\prime}(t).(2.2)

If𝔄1,2{\mathfrak{A}}_{1,2}denote the corresponding transfer matrices, then we have𝔄2​(t,z)=𝔄1​(γ​(t),z).{\mathfrak{A}}_{2}(t,z)={\mathfrak{A}}_{1}(\gamma(t),z).

In particular usingγ​(t)=∫0ttr​H​(s)​𝑑s\gamma(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\text{\rm tr}H(s)ds

shows:

## Lemma 2.2.

LetH∈ℍH\in{\mathbb{H}}. Then there always exists a trace-normalizedH^∈ℍ^\hat{H}\in\hat{\mathbb{H}}withH∼H^H\sim\hat{H}.

ForH∈ℍH\in{\mathbb{H}}, it follows directly from (2.1) that fort≥0t\geq 0𝒦H​(t,z,w):=𝔄H​(t,z)​J​𝔄H​(t,w)∗−Jz−w¯=∫0t𝔄H​(s,z)​H​(s)​𝔄H​(s,w)∗​𝑑s.\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{H}(t,z,w):=\frac{{\mathfrak{A}}_{H}(t,z)J{\mathfrak{A}}_{H}(t,w)^{*}-J}{z-\overline{w}}=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathfrak{A}}_{H}(s,z)H(s){\mathfrak{A}}_{H}(s,w)^{*}ds.(2.3)

In particular,H≥0H\geq 0implies that𝒦H∈ℂ​𝔻​(2){\mathcal{K}}_{H}\in{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{D}}(2).

AHerglotz functionis an analytic function fromℂ+{\mathbb{C}}_{+}toℂ+{\mathbb{C}}_{+}. The set of all analytic mapsw:ℂ+→ℂ+¯=ℂ+∪ℝ∪{∞}w:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}={\mathbb{C}}_{+}\cup{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}is denoted by𝒩0{\mathcal{N}}_{0}. Ifw∈𝒩0∖{∞}w\in{\mathcal{N}}_{0}\setminus\{\infty\}, then there existsα∈ℝ,β≥0\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}},\beta\geq 0and a positive Borel measureσ\sigmawith∫d​σ​(x)1+x2<∞\int\frac{d\sigma(x)}{1+x^{2}}<\infty,
such thatw​(z)=α+β​z+∫ℝ(1x−z−x1+x2)​𝑑σ​(x).\displaystyle w(z)=\alpha+\beta z+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{1}{x-z}-\frac{x}{1+x^{2}}\right)d\sigma(x).(2.4)

Write𝔄H=(𝔄11𝔄12𝔄21𝔄22){\mathfrak{A}}_{H}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{\mathfrak{A}}_{11}&{\mathfrak{A}}_{12}\\
{\mathfrak{A}}_{21}&{\mathfrak{A}}_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right). Forz∈ℂ+z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}, we define theWeyl functionofH∈ℍH\in{\mathbb{H}}bymH​(z):=limt→∞𝔄11​(t,z)​τ+𝔄12​(t,z)𝔄21​(t,z)​τ+𝔄22​(t,z)\displaystyle m_{H}(z):=\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}\frac{{\mathfrak{A}}_{11}(t,z)\tau+{\mathfrak{A}}_{12}(t,z)}{{\mathfrak{A}}_{21}(t,z)\tau+{\mathfrak{A}}_{22}(t,z)}(2.5)

where the limit does not depend onτ∈ℂ+¯\tau\in\overline{{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}since we are in the limit point case. It follows from the positivity ofHHthatmH∈𝒩0m_{H}\in{\mathcal{N}}_{0}.

## 2.2.Jacobi recursion

Let us consider a measureμ\muonℝ{\mathbb{R}}with finite moments, not supported on finitely many points, and recall how it relates to canonical systems.
The orthonormal polynomialspnp_{n}satisfy a three term recurrence relation, i.e., there exist Jacobi parametersan>0,bn∈ℝa_{n}>0,b_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}such thatz​pn​(z)\displaystyle zp_{n}(z)=an+1​pn+1​(z)+bn​pn​(z)+an​pn−1​(z),n≥1\displaystyle=a_{n+1}p_{n+1}(z)+b_{n}p_{n}(z)+a_{n}p_{n-1}(z),\quad n\geq 1(2.6)z​p0​(z)\displaystyle zp_{0}(z)=a1​p1​(z)+b0​p0​(z).\displaystyle=a_{1}p_{1}(z)+b_{0}p_{0}(z).

Forn≥1n\geq 1, the orthonormal polynomials of the second kindqnq_{n}satisfy the same recursion (2.6).
Viewing this as a second order recursion and rewriting it in matrix form leads to(−an​qn−1​(z)−qn​(z)an​pn−1​(z)pn​(z))=(−an−1​qn−2​(z)−qn−1​(z)an−1​pn−2​(z)pn−1​(z))​𝔞n​(z),\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-a_{n}q_{n-1}(z)&-q_{n}(z)\\
a_{n}p_{n-1}(z)&p_{n}(z)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-a_{n-1}q_{n-2}(z)&-q_{n-1}(z)\\
a_{n-1}p_{n-2}(z)&p_{n-1}(z)\end{pmatrix}{\mathfrak{a}}_{n}(z),(2.7)

where𝔞n​(z)=(0−1ananz−bn−1an).{\mathfrak{a}}_{n}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{1}{a_{n}}\\
a_{n}&\frac{z-b_{n-1}}{a_{n}}\end{pmatrix}.

Note that we are using row notation, where solutions of the recursion correspond to rows of the matrices in (2.7). Thus, iterating the recursion gives thenn-step transfer matrix defined by𝔄n​(z)=𝔞1​(z)​…​𝔞n​(z).\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}(z)={\mathfrak{a}}_{1}(z)\dots\mathfrak{a}_{n}(z).(2.8)

From (2.7) it follows that𝔄n​(z)=(−an​qn−1​(z)−qn​(z)an​pn−1​(z)pn​(z)).\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}-a_{n}q_{n-1}(z)&-q_{n}(z)\\
a_{n}p_{n-1}(z)&p_{n}(z)\end{pmatrix}.(2.9)

Then induction innnshows that the kernel (1.9) satisfies𝒦μ​(n,z,w)=𝔄n​(z)​J​𝔄n​(w)∗−Jz−w¯.\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{\mu}(n,z,w)=\frac{{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}(z)J{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}(w)^{*}-J}{z-\overline{w}}.(2.10)

Using𝒦μ​(n,z,z)≥0{\mathcal{K}}_{\mu}(n,z,z)\geq 0shows that𝔄n{\mathfrak{A}}_{n}is an entireJJ-inner function and hence𝒦μ​(n,⋅,⋅)∈ℂ​𝔻​(2){\mathcal{K}}_{\mu}(n,\cdot,\cdot)\in{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{D}}(2).

Let us relate this to canonical systems.
Forξ∈ℝ\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}, letW​(n,z)=W​(n,z,ξ)=𝔄n​(z+ξ)​𝔄n​(ξ)−1.W(n,z)=W(n,z,\xi)={\mathfrak{A}}_{n}(z+\xi){\mathfrak{A}}_{n}(\xi)^{-1}.

Since𝔞n+1​(z+ξ)​𝔞n+1​(ξ)−1=I−z​N,where​N=(0010),{\mathfrak{a}}_{n+1}(z+\xi){\mathfrak{a}}_{n+1}(\xi)^{-1}=I-zN,\quad\text{where }N=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},

a direct computation shows that(W​(n+1,z)−W​(n,z))​J=z​W​(n,z)​Hξ​(n),\displaystyle(W(n+1,z)-W(n,z))J=zW(n,z)H_{\xi}(n),(2.11)

whereHξ​(n)=(qn​(ξ)2−pn​(ξ)​qn​(ξ)−pn​(ξ)​qn​(ξ)pn​(ξ)2).H_{\xi}(n)=\begin{pmatrix}q_{n}(\xi)^{2}&-p_{n}(\xi)q_{n}(\xi)\\
-p_{n}(\xi)q_{n}(\xi)&p_{n}(\xi)^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

This can be extended to a canonical system by linear interpolation: Fort≥0t\geq 0defineW​(t,z)=W​(⌊t⌋,z)+(t−⌊t⌋)​(W​(⌊t⌋+1,z)−W​(⌊t⌋,z))W(t,z)=W(\lfloor t\rfloor,z)+(t-\lfloor t\rfloor)(W(\lfloor t\rfloor+1,z)-W(\lfloor t\rfloor,z))

Moreover, ifμ\muis determinate, this canonical system is in the limit point case, and ifmHξm_{H_{\xi}}is the Herglotz function associated toHξH_{\xi}by (2.5) andmμm_{\mu}as in (1.5), then it holds thatmHξ​(z−ξ)=mμ​(z).\displaystyle m_{H_{\xi}}(z-\xi)=m_{\mu}(z).

## 2.3.Continuity properties

The topologies on𝒩0{\mathcal{N}}_{0}andℍ^\hat{\mathbb{H}}defined below are chosen so that the mapH↦mHH\mapsto m_{H}is continuous.

LetΩ⊂ℂn\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{n}be open and nonempty andSnS_{n},n∈ℕ0n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}, be compact such thatSn⊂int⁡Sn+1S_{n}\subset\operatorname{int}S_{n+1},∪nSn=Ω\cup_{n}S_{n}=\Omega. Moreover, letYYbe a metric space. Then the topology of locally uniform convergence onC​(Ω,Y)C(\Omega,Y)is metrizable, with one choice of metric given byd​(f,g):=∑n=1∞2−n​min⁡{1,supz∈SndY​(f​(z),g​(z))},f,g∈C​(Ω,Y).\displaystyle d(f,g):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2^{-n}\min\{1,\sup_{z\in S_{n}}d_{Y}(f(z),g(z))\},\quad f,g\in C(\Omega,Y).

Note that𝒩0⊂C​(ℂ+,ℂ¯){\mathcal{N}}_{0}\subset C({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\overline{{\mathbb{C}}}), where the Riemann sphere is endowed with the chordal metric. We equip𝒩0{\mathcal{N}}_{0}with the subspace topology of locally uniform convergence inC​(ℂ+,ℂ¯)C({\mathbb{C}}_{+},\overline{{\mathbb{C}}}). In this way,𝒩0{\mathcal{N}}_{0}becomes a compact metric space.

Likewise, we equipℍ^\hat{\mathbb{H}}with the metricd​(H1,H2):=∑n=1∞2−n​min⁡{1,supT∈[0,n]‖∫0TH1​(t)​𝑑t−∫0TH2​(t)​𝑑t‖}\displaystyle d(H_{1},H_{2}):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2^{-n}\min\left\{1,\sup_{T\in[0,n]}\left\|\int_{0}^{T}H_{1}(t)dt-\int_{0}^{T}H_{2}(t)dt\right\|\right\}

forH1,H2∈ℍ^H_{1},H_{2}\in\hat{\mathbb{H}}.

It is a deep theorem of de Branges, that the mapH↦mHH\mapsto m_{H}is a bijection. The original proof of this result was published in the papers[26, Theorem VI],[25, Theorem XI, XII]. For a book treatment see[70, Theorem 5.1]. It is then an easy consequence of the definition of the topologies that the map is in fact a homeomorphism[70, Corollary 5.8].

## Theorem 2.3.

The map{ℍ^→𝒩0H↦mH\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hat{\mathbb{H}}&\to&{\mathcal{N}}_{0}\\[5.69054pt]
H&\mapsto&m_{H}\end{array}\right.

is a homeomorphism. In particular,ℍ^\hat{\mathbb{H}}is a compact metric space.

Consider𝔄H,𝒦H{\mathfrak{A}}_{H},{\mathcal{K}}_{H}as elements ofC​([0,∞)×ℂ,ℂ2×2)C([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}}^{2\times 2})andC​([0,∞)×ℂ×ℂ,ℂ2×2)C([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}}^{2\times 2}), respectively. Then with the corresponding topologies, standard ODE arguments show that the mapsH↦𝔄HandH↦𝒦H\displaystyle H\mapsto{\mathfrak{A}}_{H}\quad\text{and}\quad H\mapsto{\mathcal{K}}_{H}(2.12)

are continuous.

## 2.4.Rescalings

ForH∈ℍH\in{\mathbb{H}}we writeH​(t)=(h1​(t)h3​(t)h3​(t)h2​(t)).H(t)=\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}(t)&h_{3}(t)\\
h_{3}(t)&h_{2}(t)\end{pmatrix}.

We discuss the following transformation onHH.

## Definition 2.4.

Fixg​(r):(0,∞)→(0,∞)g(r):(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)and let𝒱{\mathcal{V}}be as in (1.15). We define a mapℱr:ℍ→ℍ{\mathcal{F}}_{r}:{\mathbb{H}}\to{\mathbb{H}}by(ℱr​H)​(t):=𝒱​(g​(r))​H​(r​t)​𝒱​(g​(r))∗=(g​(r)​h1​(r​t)h3​(r​t)h3​(r​t)1g​(r)​h2​(r​t)).({\mathcal{F}}_{r}H)(t):={\mathcal{V}}(g(r))H(rt){\mathcal{V}}(g(r))^{*}=\begin{pmatrix}g(r)h_{1}(rt)&h_{3}(rt)\\
h_{3}(rt)&\frac{1}{g(r)}h_{2}(rt)\end{pmatrix}.

It can be explicitly computed how this transformation acts on all the above mentioned objects.

## Lemma 2.5.

Letr>0r>0andHHbe a Hamiltonian on[0,∞)[0,\infty). Then we have𝔄ℱr​H​(t,z)\displaystyle{\mathfrak{A}}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{r}H}(t,z)=𝒱​(g​(r))​𝔄H​(t​r,z/r)​𝒱​(g​(r))−1,\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(g(r)){\mathfrak{A}}_{H}(tr,z/r){\mathcal{V}}(g(r))^{-1},𝒦ℱr​H​(t,z,w)\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{r}H}(t,z,w)=1r​𝒱​(g​(r))​𝒦H​(t​r,z/r,w/r)​𝒱​(g​(r))∗,\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}{\mathcal{V}}(g(r)){\mathcal{K}}_{H}(tr,z/r,w/r){\mathcal{V}}(g(r))^{*},mℱr​H​(z)\displaystyle m_{{\mathcal{F}}_{r}H}(z)=g​(r)​mH​(z/r).\displaystyle=g(r)m_{H}(z/r).

## Proof.

The formula for𝔄ℱr​H​(t,z){\mathfrak{A}}_{{\mathcal{F}}_{r}H}(t,z)follows by verification of (2.1). All other identies can be checked directly.
∎

## 3.Periodic modulation of power law canonical systems

Letβ∈(−1,1)\beta\in(-1,1)andρ>1\rho>1. In this section we discuss Herglotz functionsmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}of the formmβ,ω​(z):=zβ​ω​(z),\displaystyle m_{\beta,\omega}(z):=z^{\beta}\omega(z),(3.1)

whereβ∈(−1,1)\beta\in(-1,1)andω\omegaobeysω​(ρ​z)=ω​(z)\omega(\rho z)=\omega(z).
In particular, we will provide an explicit construction leading to such Herglotz functions.

## 3.1.Self-similar Herglotz functions and kernels

## Lemma 3.1.

Fixρ>1\rho>1andβ∈(−1,1)\beta\in(-1,1). A functionm:ℂ+→ℂm:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}obeysm​(ρ​z)=ρβ​m​(z),∀z∈ℂ+m(\rho z)=\rho^{\beta}m(z),\qquad\forall z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}(3.2)

if and only if it is of the formm​(z)=zβ​ω​(z)m(z)=z^{\beta}\omega(z)for someω:ℂ+→ℂ\omega:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}obeying (1.16).

## Proof.

Ifmmobeys (3.2), takingω​(z)=z−β​m​(z)\omega(z)=z^{-\beta}m(z)givesω​(ρ​z)=m​(ρ​z)ρβ​zβ=ρβ​m​(z)ρβ​zβ=ω​(z).\omega(\rho z)=\frac{m(\rho z)}{\rho^{\beta}z^{\beta}}=\frac{\rho^{\beta}m(z)}{\rho^{\beta}z^{\beta}}=\omega(z).

The other implication is similar.
∎

forg​(s)=sβg(s)=s^{\beta}.

For the study of zeros, we need one more fact:

## Lemma 3.3.

Assume thatmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}does not have an analytic continuation toℂ∖(−∞,0]{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(-\infty,0]which is real-valued on(0,∞)(0,\infty). Then(𝒦1)22​(⋅,0)({\mathcal{K}}_{1})_{22}(\cdot,0)has infinitely many zeros on(0,∞)(0,\infty).

## Proof.

Assume that(𝒦1)22​(⋅,0)({\mathcal{K}}_{1})_{22}(\cdot,0)has finitely many zeros. We will use that𝒦ρn=𝒦1{\mathcal{K}}_{\rho^{n}}={\mathcal{K}}_{1}. Note that(1.20)implies thatt​(ρn)t(\rho^{n})is increasing andt​(ρn)→∞t(\rho^{n})\to\inftyasn→∞n\to\infty. The relation (1.21) implies(𝒦1)22​(z,w)=𝒦22​(t​(1),z,w)=𝒦22​(t​(ρn),z/ρn,w/ρn)ρn​(1+β)({\mathcal{K}}_{1})_{22}(z,w)={\mathcal{K}}_{22}(t(1),z,w)=\frac{{\mathcal{K}}_{22}(t(\rho^{n}),z/\rho^{n},w/\rho^{n})}{\rho^{n(1+\beta)}}

Letξm\xi_{m}denote the largest zero of(𝒦1)22​(⋅,0)({\mathcal{K}}_{1})_{22}(\cdot,0)on(0,∞)(0,\infty). Then, for anyϵ>0\epsilon>0, fornnlarge enough𝒦22​(t​(ρn),⋅,0){\mathcal{K}}_{22}(t(\rho^{n}),\cdot,0)has no zeros in(ϵ,∞)(\epsilon,\infty). Let𝔄​(t,z){\mathfrak{A}}(t,z)denote the transfer matrix generating𝒦{\mathcal{K}}by (2.3). An interlacing property as in the discussion preceding Theorem 10.1 in[37]shows that𝔄22​(t​(ρn),⋅){\mathfrak{A}}_{22}(t(\rho^{n}),\cdot)has at most one zero in(ϵ,∞)(\epsilon,\infty). On the other hand choosingτ=∞\tau=\inftyin (2.5) shows thatmβ,ω​(z)=limn→∞𝔄11​(t​(ρn),z)𝔄22​(t​(ρn),z).m_{\beta,\omega}(z)=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{{\mathfrak{A}}_{11}(t(\rho^{n}),z)}{{\mathfrak{A}}_{22}(t(\rho^{n}),z)}.

Herglotz function arguments imply thatmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}has a meromorphic continuation toℂ∖(−∞,ϵ]{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(-\infty,\epsilon]with the symmetrymβ,ω​(z¯)=mβ,ω​(z)¯m_{\beta,\omega}(\bar{z})=\overline{m_{\beta,\omega}(z)}and with at most one pole on(ϵ,∞)(\epsilon,\infty). Sinceϵ>0\epsilon>0was arbitrary it can be replaced by0. Ifmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}has a pole atλ>0\lambda>0, it would also have a pole atρn​λ\rho^{n}\lambda, contradicting the uniqueness of the positive pole. Thus, this continuation is analytic onℂ∖(−∞,0]{\mathbb{C}}\setminus(-\infty,0].
∎

## 3.2.Rescaling limits

The aim of this subsection is to prove rescaling limits for the matrix Christoffel-Darboux kernel associated to Herglotz functions which are asymptotically close to somemβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}. We begin by reinterpreting a statement about the normal behavior of a Herglotz function as a convergence statement in𝒩0{\mathcal{N}}_{0}.

## Lemma 3.4.

Letw∈𝒩0w\in{\mathcal{N}}_{0}. Letβ∈(−1,1),ρ>1\beta\in(-1,1),\rho>1andω:ℂ+→ℂ∖{0}\omega:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}obey (1.16). Letmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}be given by (3.1). Thenlimy→0|e−i​β​π/2​y−β​w​(i​y)−ω​(i​y)|=0\lim\limits_{y\to 0}\lvert e^{-i\beta\pi/2}y^{-\beta}w(iy)-\omega(iy)\rvert=0(3.4)

if and only if uniformly on compact subsets ofℂ+{\mathbb{C}}_{+}limn→∞w​(z​ρ−n)​ρn​β=mβ,ω​(z).\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}w(z\rho^{-n})\rho^{n\beta}=m_{\beta,\omega}(z).(3.5)

In particular, if this holds, thenmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}is a Herglotz function.

## Proof.

We writern=ρnr_{n}=\rho^{n}.

⇒\Rightarrow: By compactness and analyticity it suffices to show that fory>0y>0limn→∞|w​(i​y/rn)​rnβ−mβ,ω​(i​y)|=0.\displaystyle\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}|w(iy/r_{n})r_{n}^{\beta}-m_{\beta,\omega}(iy)|=0.(3.6)

Setξn=y/rn\xi_{n}=y/r_{n}. Then using thatω​(i​ξn)=ω​(i​y)\omega(i\xi_{n})=\omega(iy), we get|w​(i​y/rn)​rnβ−mβ,ω​(i​y)|\displaystyle|w(iy/r_{n})r_{n}^{\beta}-m_{\beta,\omega}(iy)|=|w​(i​ξn)​(i​y/i​ξn)β−(i​y)β​ω​(i​ξn)|\displaystyle=|w(i\xi_{n})(iy/i\xi_{n})^{\beta}-(iy)^{\beta}\omega(i\xi_{n})|=yβ​|w​(i​ξn)​(i​ξn)−β−ω​(i​ξn)|.\displaystyle=y^{\beta}|w(i\xi_{n})(i\xi_{n})^{-\beta}-\omega(i\xi_{n})|.

Hence, by assumption we get (3.6).

⇐\Leftarrow: Takingz=i​tz=itwitht∈[1,ρ]t\in[1,\rho], we have uniform convergencelimn→∞w​(i​t​ρ−n)​ρn​β=(i​t)β​ω​(i​t)\lim_{n\to\infty}w(it\rho^{-n})\rho^{n\beta}=(it)^{\beta}\omega(it)

so dividing by(i​t)β(it)^{\beta}and usingω​(i​t)=ω​(i​t​ρ−n)\omega(it)=\omega(it\rho^{-n})we rewrite this aslimn→∞supt∈[1,ρ]|w​(i​t​ρ−n)​(i​t​ρ−n)−β−ω​(i​t​ρ−n)|=0.\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[1,\rho]}\lvert w(it\rho^{-n})(it\rho^{-n})^{-\beta}-\omega(it\rho^{-n})\rvert=0.

Usingy=t​ρ−ny=t\rho^{-n}this is logically equivalent to (3.4).

When (3.5) holds, it givesmβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}as a locally uniform limit of Herglotz functions; sincemβ,ωm_{\beta,\omega}is nonzero, it must be a Herglotz function.
∎

## 3.3.Fine structure of zeros from periodically modulated limits of Weyl functions

The Freud–Levin theorem[43,56]was originally stated as the result that clock behavior follows from convergence of rescaled CD kernels to the sine kernel. The proof, however, allows for significant generalizations; see[37, Section 10]for a general statement in case of convergence of rescaled kernels to an essentially arbitrary limit kernel.

For our current purposes, we need to look beyond convergence and generalize the Freud–Levin theorem to give uniform bounds under precompactness assumptions. To formulate this, consider a sequence of Hermite–Biehler functionsEnE_{n}(entire functions such that|En​(z)|>|En​(z¯)|\lvert E_{n}(z)\rvert>\lvert E_{n}(\overline{z})\rvertforz∈ℂ+z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}) with no real zeros. We use the notationE♯​(z)=E​(z¯)¯E^{\sharp}(z)=\overline{E(\overline{z})}

and consider the associated reproducing kernelsKn​(z,w)=i​En​(z)​En♯​(w¯)−En♯​(z)​En​(w¯)z−w¯K_{n}(z,w)=i\frac{E_{n}(z)E_{n}^{\sharp}(\overline{w})-E_{n}^{\sharp}(z)E_{n}(\overline{w})}{z-\overline{w}}

The functionEn+En♯E_{n}+E_{n}^{\sharp}has only real simple zeros, which we labelξj(n)\xi_{j}^{(n)}be counting fromξ\xi, so that⋯<ξ0(n)<ξ≤ξ1(n)<ξ2(n)<…\dots<\xi_{0}^{(n)}<\xi\leq\xi_{1}^{(n)}<\xi_{2}^{(n)}<\dots

In a typical application to orthogonal polynomials, we useEn=pn+i​pn−1E_{n}=p_{n}+ip_{n-1}, in which caseξj(n)\xi_{j}^{(n)}are precisely zeros ofpnp_{n}.

For a kernelKEK_{E}, if the functionKE​(⋅,0)K_{E}(\cdot,0)has infinitely many zeros on(0,∞)(0,\infty), then by interlacing, so doesKE​(⋅,λ)K_{E}(\cdot,\lambda)for anyλ∈ℝ\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}, and we denote byκ1​(λ,KE)<κ2​(λ,KE)<…\kappa_{1}(\lambda,K_{E})<\kappa_{2}(\lambda,K_{E})<\dots

all the zeros ofKE​(⋅,λ)K_{E}(\cdot,\lambda)in(λ,∞)(\lambda,\infty).

## Theorem 3.10.

Assume that for someξ∈ℝ\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}and some sequenceτn→∞\tau_{n}\to\infty, the sequence of rescaled kernelsK​(n,ξ+zτn,ξ+wτn)K​(n,ξ,ξ)\frac{K\left(n,\xi+\frac{z}{\tau_{n}},\xi+\frac{w}{\tau_{n}}\right)}{K\left(n,\xi,\xi\right)}

is precompact in the topology of uniform convergence on compacts. Denote by𝒦{\mathcal{K}}the set of its limit points. Assume that for eachKE∈𝒦K_{E}\in{\mathcal{K}}, the functionKE​(⋅,0)K_{E}(\cdot,0)has infinitely many zeros on(0,∞)(0,\infty).
- (a)lim supn→∞τn​(ξ1(n)−ξ)≤maxKE∈𝒦⁡κ1​(0,KE).\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}(\xi_{1}^{(n)}-\xi)\leq\max_{K_{E}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\kappa_{1}(0,K_{E}).(3.15)
- (b)

For every1≤k<l1\leq k<l, there exist constantsCk,l±∈(0,∞)C_{k,l}^{\pm}\in(0,\infty)such thatCk,l−≤lim infn→∞τn​(ξl(n)−ξk(n))≤lim supn→∞τn​(ξl(n)−ξk(n))≤Ck,l+.C_{k,l}^{-}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}(\xi_{l}^{(n)}-\xi_{k}^{(n)})\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}(\xi_{l}^{(n)}-\xi_{k}^{(n)})\leq C_{k,l}^{+}.(3.16)

The constants are determined by the family of limit kernels as follows:Ck,l−\displaystyle C_{k,l}^{-}=minKE∈𝒦⁡minλ∈[0,κ1​(0,KE)]⁡(κl−1​(λ,KE)−κk−1​(λ,KE))\displaystyle=\min_{K_{E}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\min_{\lambda\in[0,\kappa_{1}(0,K_{E})]}\Bigl(\kappa_{l-1}(\lambda,K_{E})-\kappa_{k-1}(\lambda,K_{E})\Bigr)(3.17)Ck,l+\displaystyle C_{k,l}^{+}=maxKE∈𝒦⁡maxλ∈[0,κ1​(0,KE)]⁡(κl−1​(λ,KE)−κk−1​(λ,KE))\displaystyle=\max_{K_{E}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\max_{\lambda\in[0,\kappa_{1}(0,K_{E})]}\Bigl(\kappa_{l-1}(\lambda,K_{E})-\kappa_{k-1}(\lambda,K_{E})\Bigr)(3.18)
- (c)

If, in addition, for eachKE∈𝒦K_{E}\in{\mathcal{K}}, the functionKE​(⋅,0)K_{E}(\cdot,0)has infinitely many zeros on(−∞,0)(-\infty,0), then (b) holds for allk<lk<linℤ{\mathbb{Z}}.

## Proof.

(a) Along a subsequencenjn_{j}such that for someKE∈𝒦K_{E}\in{\mathcal{K}},limj→∞K​(nj,ξ+zτnj,ξ+wτnj)K​(nj,ξ,ξ)=KE​(z,w),\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{K\left(n_{j},\xi+\frac{z}{\tau_{n_{j}}},\xi+\frac{w}{\tau_{n_{j}}}\right)}{K\left(n_{j},\xi,\xi\right)}=K_{E}(z,w),(3.19)

by the generalization of the Freud–Levin theorem provided in[37, Theorem 10.1],lim supj→∞τnj​(ξ1(nj)−ξ)≤κ1​(0,KE).\limsup_{j\to\infty}\tau_{n_{j}}(\xi_{1}^{(n_{j})}-\xi)\leq\kappa_{1}(0,K_{E}).

Thus, by precompactness of the rescaled kernels, (3.15) holds.

(b) First note that the constants defined in (3.17), (3.18) are well-defined by compactness of𝒦{\mathcal{K}}and the continuity of zeros (by Hurwitz’s theorem) and lie in(0,∞)(0,\infty). Take a subsequencenjn_{j}such that (3.19) holds and that for someλ∈[0,KE​(⋅,0)]\lambda\in[0,K_{E}(\cdot,0)],limj→∞τnj​(ξ1(nj)−ξ)=λ.\lim_{j\to\infty}\tau_{n_{j}}(\xi_{1}^{(n_{j})}-\xi)=\lambda.

Using compactness completes the proof.

(c) is proved analogously to (b) if the limit kernels have infinitely many negative zeros.

and2−q​ν​((3q​a,3q​b])=ν​((a,b])2^{-q}\nu((3^{q}a,3^{q}b])=\nu((a,b])for all(a,b]⊂ℝ(a,b]\subset{\mathbb{R}}. so we can define Herglotz functionsm​(z)=∫1x−z​𝑑μ​(x),f​(z)=∫1x−z​𝑑ν​(x).m(z)=\int\frac{1}{x-z}\,d\mu(x),\qquad f(z)=\int\frac{1}{x-z}\,d\nu(x).(3.20)

## 3.5.Examples in terms of comb mappings

We provide an explicit construction in terms of certain comb mappings, which leads to a Herglotz functionmmsatisfying (3.2).

First we need to construct a comb domain with a self-similarity property. To construct such a domain, we can choose in an arbitrary way parametersλ>1\lambda>1, and for each choice of sign±\pm, a sequence ofη±k∈[1,λ)\eta_{\pm}^{k}\in[1,\lambda)with no repetitions, and a sequence ofh±k>0h_{\pm}^{k}>0,1≤k≤n±1\leq k\leq n_{\pm}. Here eachn±n_{\pm}may be finite or infinite; if it is infinite we impose the conditionlimk→∞h±k=0.\lim_{k\to\infty}h_{\pm}^{k}=0.(3.21)0η+\eta_{+}λ​η+\lambda\eta_{+}η+k\eta_{+}^{k}h+kh_{+}^{k}h+h_{+}λ​h+\lambda h_{+}−η−-\eta_{-}−λ​η−-\lambda\eta_{-}h−h_{-}λ​h−\lambda h_{-}⋅λ−1\cdot\lambda^{-1}⋅λ\cdot\lambdaFigure 1.The comb-domainΠ\Pirelated to𝖤{\mathsf{E}}

Under these assumptions, the setΠ=ℂ+∖⋃n∈ℤ(⋃k=1n−(−λn​η−k,λn​(−η−k+i​h−k)]∪⋃k=1n+(λn​η+k,λn​(η+k+i​h+k)])\displaystyle\Pi={\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\bigg(\bigcup_{k=1}^{n_{-}}(-\lambda^{n}\eta_{-}^{k},\lambda^{n}(-\eta_{-}^{k}+ih_{-}^{k})]\cup\bigcup_{k=1}^{n_{+}}(\lambda^{n}\eta_{+}^{k},\lambda^{n}(\eta_{+}^{k}+ih_{+}^{k})]\bigg)(3.22)

is a comb domain with the self-similar propertyΠ=λ​Π.\displaystyle\Pi=\lambda\Pi.(3.23)

An illustration of this construction is given in Figure1. The domainΠ\Piis simply connected and due to the assumption (3.21) the boundary is locally connected. By Carathéodory’s theorem (sometimes called the continuity theorem[65, Chapter 2]) this ensures that conformal maps fromℂ+{\mathbb{C}}_{+}toΠ\Pican be continuously extended to the boundary. Since0and∞\inftyare not cut points, i.e.,∂Π∖{0}\partial\Pi\setminus\{0\}and∂Π∖{∞}\partial\Pi\setminus\{\infty\}are connected, these values are taken precisely once[65, Chapter 2]. Thus, there is a conformal mapθ:ℂ+→Π\theta:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to\Pisuch thatθ​(0)=0\theta(0)=0andθ​(∞)=∞\theta(\infty)=\infty, and it is unique up to rescaling ofℂ+{\mathbb{C}}_{+}(i.e. all other such maps are of the formθ​(ρ​z)\theta(\rho z)for someρ>0\rho>0). Sinceθ\thetacan be continuously extended to the boundary, we can define𝖤=θ−1​(ℝ)=ℝ∖⋃k∈ℤ(𝖺k,𝖻k),\displaystyle{\mathsf{E}}=\theta^{-1}({\mathbb{R}})={\mathbb{R}}\setminus\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}({\mathsf{a}}_{k},{\mathsf{b}}_{k}),(3.24)

where each gap(𝖺k,𝖻k)({\mathsf{a}}_{k},{\mathsf{b}}_{k})is the preimage of some needle of the comb.

There existsρ>λ\rho>\lambdasuch thatλ​θ​(z)=θ​(ρ​z),z∈ℂ+.\displaystyle\lambda\theta(z)=\theta(\rho z),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.(3.25)

To prove thatρ>λ\rho>\lambda, it suffices to consider the simplest configuration with only one slit extended by a geometric progression (the casen−+n+=1n_{-}+n_{+}=1). Indeed, for a general pattern, letΠ^\hat{\Pi}be the domain with a single generating slit atη+1\eta^{1}_{+}of the heighth+1h^{1}_{+}and the same multiplierλ\lambda(ifn+=0n_{+}=0, useη−1\eta^{1}_{-}andh−1h^{1}_{-}instead). Denote byθ^:ℂ+→Π^\hat{\theta}:{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\to\hat{\Pi}the corresponding conformal map. ThenΠ⊂Π^\Pi\subset\hat{\Pi}, so the functionw​(z)=θ^−1​(θ​(z))w(z)=\hat{\theta}^{-1}(\theta(z))

mapsℂ+{\mathbb{C}}_{+}into itself. A Herglotz function can grow at most aszz. withβ≤β^<0\beta\leq\hat{\beta}<0.

so(λρ)n=Im​θ​(i)a+∫d​σ​(x)x2+ρ−2​n≤Im​θ​(i)a+∫01d​σ​(x)x2+ρ−2​n.\left(\frac{\lambda}{\rho}\right)^{n}=\frac{\text{\rm Im\ }\theta(i)}{a+\int\frac{d\sigma(x)}{x^{2}+\rho^{-2n}}}\leq\frac{\text{\rm Im\ }\theta(i)}{a+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d\sigma(x)}{x^{2}+\rho^{-2n}}}.

By monotonicity we can pass to the limitlimn→∞(λρ)n≤Im​θ​(i)a+∫01d​σ​(x)x2.\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\rho}\right)^{n}\leq\frac{\text{\rm Im\ }\theta(i)}{a+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d\sigma(x)}{x^{2}}}.

We estimate the last integral: Sinceθ\thetacan be continuously extended toℝ{\mathbb{R}}, Stieltjes inversion[60, Proposition 7.43]shows thatσ\sigmais absolutely continuous with densityd​σ​(x)=1π​Im​θ​(x)​d​x.\displaystyle d\sigma(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\text{\rm Im\ }\theta(x)dx.(3.26)

Letη0,h0\eta_{0},h_{0}be the data ofΠ\Pias in (3.22) and(𝖺0,𝖻0)=θ−1​((η0,η0+i​h0])({\mathsf{a}}_{0},{\mathsf{b}}_{0})=\theta^{-1}((\eta_{0},\eta_{0}+ih_{0}])and assume without loss of generality that(𝖺0,𝖻0)⊂[0,1]({\mathsf{a}}_{0},{\mathsf{b}}_{0})\subset[0,1]. From (3.26) we conclude thatσ\sigmais supported in the gaps𝖺k,𝖻k{\mathsf{a}}_{k},{\mathsf{b}}_{k}.

The functionIm​θ\text{\rm Im\ }\thetais a Martin function forℂ∖𝖤{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathsf{E}}[39]. That is, it is a positive harmonic function inℂ∖𝖤{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathsf{E}}which extends to a subharmonic function toℂ{\mathbb{C}}vanishing on𝖤{\mathsf{E}}. Using the Hadamard representation for subharmonic functions[46, Section 4.2], yields that there exists a measureν\nusupported on𝖤{\mathsf{E}}withIm​θ​(z)=a​Re​z+b+∫|x|≤1log⁡|x−z|​d​ν​(x)\displaystyle\text{\rm Im\ }\theta(z)=a\text{\rm Re\ }z+b+\int_{|x|\leq 1}\log|x-z|d\nu(x)+∫|x|>1(log⁡|1−zx|+Re​zx)​𝑑ν​(x).\displaystyle+\int_{|x|>1}\left(\log\left|1-\frac{z}{x}\right|+\frac{\text{\rm Re\ }z}{x}\right)d\nu(x).(3.27)

By this integral representation,Im​θ\text{\rm Im\ }\thetais concave in gaps and thus we can estimate∫𝖺k𝖻kd​σ​(x)x2≥1𝖻k2​∫𝖺k𝖻k𝑑σ​(x)≥(𝖻k−𝖺k)​hk2​𝖻k2=(𝖻0−𝖺0)​ρk​h0​λk2​𝖻02​ρ2​k=C​λkρk,\int_{{\mathsf{a}}_{k}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{k}}\frac{d\sigma(x)}{x^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{{\mathsf{b}}_{k}^{2}}\int_{{\mathsf{a}}_{k}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{k}}d\sigma(x)\geq\frac{({\mathsf{b}}_{k}-{\mathsf{a}}_{k})h_{k}}{2{\mathsf{b}}_{k}^{2}}=\frac{({\mathsf{b}}_{0}-{\mathsf{a}}_{0})\rho^{k}h_{0}\lambda^{k}}{2{\mathsf{b}}_{0}^{2}\rho^{2k}}=C\frac{\lambda^{k}}{\rho^{k}},

whereC:=(𝖻0−𝖺0)​h02​𝖻02C:=\frac{({\mathsf{b}}_{0}-{\mathsf{a}}_{0})h_{0}}{2{\mathsf{b}}_{0}^{2}}. Noting that for the interval(0,1)(0,1)we have to take negativekk, we get∫01d​σ​(x)x2≥C​∑k=0∞(ρλ)k.\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d\sigma(x)}{x^{2}}\geq C\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\rho}{\lambda}\right)^{k}.

That is,limn→∞(λρ)n≤Im​θ​(i)a+C​∑k=0∞(ρλ)k.\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\rho}\right)^{n}\leq\frac{\text{\rm Im\ }\theta(i)}{a+C\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\rho}{\lambda}\right)^{k}}.

Since the right-hand side is finite, we conclude thatλ≤ρ\lambda\leq\rho. Hence, the sum diverges and the right-hand side is0. We conclude that the limit on the left-hand side is0, i.e.,λ<ρ\lambda<\rho.

It remains to show thatm∈𝒩0m\in{\mathcal{N}}_{0}. Recall the definition of𝖤{\mathsf{E}}in (3.24). ## Proposition 3.13.

It holds that∫d​μ𝖤​(x)1+|x|<∞\int\frac{d\mu_{{\mathsf{E}}}(x)}{1+|x|}<\infty(3.30)

andm​(z)=m𝖤​(z)=∫𝖤1x−z​𝑑μ𝖤​(x).\displaystyle m(z)=m_{{\mathsf{E}}}(z)=\int_{\mathsf{E}}\frac{1}{x-z}\,d\mu_{\mathsf{E}}(x).(3.31)

In particularm∈𝒩0m\in{\mathcal{N}}_{0}.

## Proof.

We start again from the Hadamard representation (3.5) of the subharmonic functionθ\theta. Adding the harmonic conjugate forz∈ℂ+z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{+}, differentiating and passing from normalization at0to normalization atiiyieldsi​θ′​(z)=a+∫𝖤(1x−z−x1+x2)​𝑑ν​(x).i\theta^{\prime}(z)=a+\int_{{\mathsf{E}}}\left(\frac{1}{x-z}-\frac{x}{1+x^{2}}\right)\,d\nu(x).(3.32)

Now Stieltjes inversion yields thatν=μ𝖤\nu=\mu_{\mathsf{E}}.
It remains to show that (3.30) holds andi​θ′​(z)i\theta^{\prime}(z)can in fact be represented as in (3.31). For this, note thatIm​i​θ′​(i​y)≤|i​θ′​(i​y)|≤C​yβ\text{\rm Im\ }i\theta^{\prime}(iy)\leq|i\theta^{\prime}(iy)|\leq Cy^{\beta}, forβ<0\beta<0by Corollary3.12. Now both claims follow from the classical connection between growth of Herglotz functions and tails of the measure, cf. e.g.[60, Proposition 7.33].

## 4.Expanding polynomials with real Julia sets

## 4.1.Limit cycles for balanced measures on Julia sets

As an application of our main theorem, we show that Theorem3.6can be applied to orthogonal polynomials with respect to the balanced measure on the Julia set of an arbitrary expanding polynomialTTwith a real Julia set𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0}, and rescaled at an arbitrary periodic pointξ\xiofTT. Let𝖺=min⁡𝖤0{\mathsf{a}}=\min{\mathsf{E}}_{0}and𝖻=max⁡𝖤0{\mathsf{b}}=\max{\mathsf{E}}_{0}. Such a polynomial has the following characteristic property: the critical points𝒞T={c:T′​(c)=0}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}=\{c:T^{\prime}(c)=0\}

are real and simple, and the critical valuesT​(c)T(c)are such thatT​(c)∈ℝ∖[𝖺,𝖻].T(c)\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus[{\mathsf{a}},{\mathsf{b}}].

That is, all critical points belong to the basin of attraction of infinity, see[40, Theorem 2.19, p. 584],T∘n​(c)→∞,∀c∈𝒞T,T^{\circ n}(c)\to\infty,\quad\forall c\in{\mathcal{C}}_{T},

whereT∘(n+1)​(z)=T​(T∘n​(z))T^{\circ(n+1)}(z)=T(T^{\circ n}(z)).

In this subsection we prove Theorem1.6. At first, let us assume thatξ\xiis a fixed or periodic point. It suffices, by replacingTTby some iterateT∘mT^{\circ m}, to consider the case whenT​(ξ)=ξ,T′​(ξ)>0.T(\xi)=\xi,\qquad T^{\prime}(\xi)>0.

It is known that[32, Theorem 4.2]thenT′​(ξ)≥dT^{\prime}(\xi)\geq d. By conjugating with a translation, we assume without loss of generality thatξ=0\xi=0, i.e.,T​(0)=0T(0)=0. LetT′​(0)=ρ>1T^{\prime}(0)=\rho>1.

The claim of Theorem1.6follows mainly from two classical functional equations in iteration theory.
First, the following limit exists, see e.g.[44, Sect. 3],F​(z)=limn→∞Fn​(z),Fn​(z)=T∘n​(z/ρn).F(z)=\lim_{n\to\infty}F_{n}(z),\quad F_{n}(z)=T^{\circ n}(z/\rho^{n}).(4.1)

The limit functionF​(z)F(z)satisfies the Poincaré equation (1.28). Note thatFn​(0)=0,Fn′​(0)=1F_{n}(0)=0,\ F_{n}^{\prime}(0)=1, respectivelyFFis normalized by the following conditionsF​(0)=0,F′​(0)=1F(0)=0,\ F^{\prime}(0)=1.

Similar to the construction in Section3.5, there exists a comb mappingθ0\theta_{0}such that the Julia set𝖤0{\mathsf{E}}_{0}as defined in (1.25) is given byθ0−1​([0,π])\theta_{0}^{-1}([0,\pi]). In this section, we work with the Green’s mappingg𝖤0​(z)=ei​θ0​(z)g_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}(z)=e^{i\theta_{0}(z)}; cf.[66].
Note that in the iteration theory this is the well known Böttcher function, see e.g.[44, Sect. 4].
Initially only defined onℂ+{\mathbb{C}}_{+},g𝖤0g_{{\mathsf{E}}_{0}}can be extended to the complex Green function with respect to∞\inftyto the domainℂ¯∖𝖤0\bar{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathsf{E}}_{0}. It is a multivalued (character automorphic) function in the domain. We use the normalizationθ0​(0)=0\theta_{0}(0)=0. Sinceei​θ0​(T​(z))e^{i\theta_{0}(T(z))}on the universal covering is again a Blaschke product with zeros corresponding to∞\inftyof multiplicitydd, we have the Böttcher equationei​θ0​(T​(z))=ed​i​θ0​(z).e^{i\theta_{0}(T(z))}=e^{di\theta_{0}(z)}.(4.2)

By (4.1), the following limit existsθ​(z)=limn→∞θ0​(T∘n​(z/ρn))=θ0​(F​(z)).\theta(z)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\theta_{0}(T^{\circ n}(z/\rho^{n}))=\theta_{0}(F(z)).(4.3)

and thereforeθ​(ρ​z)=d​θ​(z).\theta(\rho z)=d\theta(z).

Thus we proved that three entries of the matrices on the LHS and RHS of
(5.12) coincide. Moreover they are not vanishing identically. Since the determinants of both matrices are one,
the identity
(5.12) is proved.
∎

Now we can show (5.16) by induction. Since𝒞~1={−ρ/2}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}=\{-\rho/2\}, the statement is true forn=1n=1. Now takec∈𝒞n+1c\in{\mathcal{C}}_{n+1}. Ifc∈T−1​(𝒞~n)c\in T^{-1}(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{n}), thenT​(c)∈𝒞~nT(c)\in\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{n}and thus, by induction hypothesis,T∘n​(T​(c))=T∘(n+1)​(c)T^{\circ n}(T(c))=T^{\circ(n+1)}(c)belongs also to this set. This finishes the proof of (5.16). It follows from (5.16) again by induction that|Fn​(c)+ρ/2|≥ρ2/4−ρ/2,∀c∈𝒞n|F_{n}(c)+\rho/2|\geq\rho^{2}/4-\rho/2,\quad\forall c\in{\mathcal{C}}_{n}

and hence0<−m𝒥,k−​(Fn​(c))Fn​(c)<M.0<-\frac{m_{{\mathcal{J}},k}^{-}(F_{n}(c))}{F_{n}(c)}<M.

(iii): SinceFn→FF_{n}\to F, by Hurwitz theorem and local uniform convergence, we have that for everyc∈𝒞c\in{\mathcal{C}}there is a uniquec(n)∈𝒞nc^{(n)}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{n}such thatFn​(c(n))→F​(c)F_{n}(c^{(n)})\to F(c). ## Theorem 5.7.

Let𝔅k​(z)=ℱ​(z)−1​𝔄k​(F​(z))​ℱ​(z)​(10U^k​(z)1),ℱ=(100F′​(z)),{\mathfrak{B}}_{k}(z)={\mathcal{F}}(z)^{-1}{\mathfrak{A}}_{k}(F(z)){\mathcal{F}}(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\

Thenlimn→∞1|ρ|n​𝒰n​𝒦k​2n​(z/ρn)​𝒰n∗=𝒦^k​(z).\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\rho|^{n}}{\mathcal{U}}_{n}{\mathcal{K}}_{k2^{n}}(z/\rho^{n}){\mathcal{U}}_{n}^{*}=\hat{\mathcal{K}}_{k}(z).(5.24)

Sincelimk→∞𝔄k​(z)⋆0=m𝒥​(z),\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}{\mathfrak{A}}_{k}(z)\star 0=m_{{\mathcal{J}}}(z),

we get the first equality in (5.22). The second equality follows from (4.7). Finally,Fn→FF_{n}\to Fand Lemma5.6, allow us to take the limit in (5.12) to obtain (5.24).

It remains to show that the upper and lower limits coincide. The proof will be given in two steps. First, in Lemma5.10, we find an expression for a possible jump matrix (5.30). ## Lemma 5.15(Main Lemma).

ForΦ2≠0\Phi_{2}\not=0along the suitable subsequenceIϰ′I^{\prime}_{\varkappa}the following limit holds1Φ22​lims→∞Rks​(F​(z/ρs))​F′​(z/ρs)/ρs=(mℓ−​(z)1−m0+​mℓ−​(z)−1mℓ+​(z)−m0+)−1\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Phi_{2}^{2}}\lim_{s\to\infty}R_{k_{s}}(F(z/\rho^{s}))F^{\prime}(z/\rho^{s})/\rho^{s}=\left(\frac{m^{-}_{\ell}(z)}{1-m_{0}^{+}m^{-}_{\ell}(z)}-\frac{1}{m^{+}_{\ell}(z)-m_{0}^{+}}\right)^{-1}(5.45)

whereRks​(z)R_{k_{s}}(z)is theksk_{s}-th diagonal entry of the resolvent matrix(𝒥−z)−1({\mathcal{J}}-z)^{-1}.

## Proof.

We recall that the resolvent diagonal entry can be represented in terms of one-sided resolvent functionsm𝒥,k±m_{{\mathcal{J}},k}^{\pm}(5.3).
By adding (5.42) and (5.44) we obtain (5.45).

## Theorem 5.16.

where we used the Stieljes inversion formula for the Herglotz functionRϰ​(u)R_{\varkappa}(u)in the last step.

## Appendix AMonotonicity ofMM-type

For the reader’s convenience we provide a proof of a well known lemma.

## Lemma A.1.

Assume thatlimy→∞log⁡yM​(i​y)=0.\lim_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log y}{M(iy)}=0.(A.1)

Thenlim supy→∞log⁡‖𝔄​(i​y)‖M​(i​y)=lim supy→∞log⁡‖𝔄​(i​y)​J​𝔄​(i​y)∗−J‖2​M​(i​y).\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log\|{\mathfrak{A}}(iy)\|}{M(iy)}=\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log\|{\mathfrak{A}}(iy)J{\mathfrak{A}}(iy)^{*}-J\|}{2M(iy)}.(A.2)

## Proof.

We use the following change of variablesA​(ζ)=V∗​𝔄​(z)​V,V=12​(i−i11),z=i​1+ζ1−ζ.A(\zeta)=V^{*}{\mathfrak{A}}(z)V,\quad V=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}i&-i\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad z=i\frac{1+\zeta}{1-\zeta}.

In this case‖𝔄​(z)‖=‖A​(ζ)‖and‖𝔄​(z)​J​𝔄​(z)∗−J‖=‖A​(ζ)​𝔧​A​(ζ)∗−𝔧‖.\|{\mathfrak{A}}(z)\|=\|A(\zeta)\|\quad\text{and}\quad\|{\mathfrak{A}}(z)J{\mathfrak{A}}(z)^{*}-J\|=\|A(\zeta){\mathfrak{j}}A(\zeta)^{*}-{\mathfrak{j}}\|.

The matrix functionA​(ζ)=(a11​(ζ)a12​(ζ)a21​(ζ)a22​(ζ)),A(\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}(\zeta)&a_{12}(\zeta)\\
a_{21}(\zeta)&a_{22}(\zeta)\end{pmatrix},

is𝔧{\mathfrak{j}}-expanding in𝔻{\mathbb{D}},Γ​(ζ)=(γ11​(ζ)γ12​(ζ)γ21​(ζ)γ22​(ζ))=A​(ζ)​𝔧​A​(ζ)∗−𝔧≥0.\Gamma(\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{11}(\zeta)&\gamma_{12}(\zeta)\\
\gamma_{21}(\zeta)&\gamma_{22}(\zeta)\end{pmatrix}=A(\zeta){\mathfrak{j}}A(\zeta)^{*}-{\mathfrak{j}}\geq 0.(A.3)

WLOG we assume thatA​(ζ)A(\zeta)meets the Arov normalizationa21​(0)=0a_{21}(0)=0(the Arov gauge[14]).

Diagonal entries of the inequality (A.3) provides the following estimations−|a11​(ζ)|2+|a12​(ζ)|2+1\displaystyle-|a_{11}(\zeta)|^{2}+|a_{12}(\zeta)|^{2}+1≥0\displaystyle\geq 0−|a21​(ζ)|2+|a22​(ζ)|2−1\displaystyle-|a_{21}(\zeta)|^{2}+|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}-1≥0.\displaystyle\geq 0.

In addition (A.3) anddetA​(ζ)≠0\det A(\zeta)\not=0impliesA​(ζ)∗​𝔧​A​(ζ)−𝔧≥0A(\zeta)^{*}{\mathfrak{j}}A(\zeta)-{\mathfrak{j}}\geq 0and therefore−|a12​(ζ)|2+|a22​(ζ)|2−1≥0.-|a_{12}(\zeta)|^{2}+|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}-1\geq 0.

Thus|a11​(ζ)|2≤|a12​(ζ)|2+1\displaystyle|a_{11}(\zeta)|^{2}\leq|a_{12}(\zeta)|^{2}+1≤|a22​(ζ)|2\displaystyle\leq|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}|a21​(ζ)|2≤|a22​(ζ)|2−1\displaystyle|a_{21}(\zeta)|^{2}\leq|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}-1≤|a22​(ζ)|2\displaystyle\leq|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}|a12​(ζ)|2≤|a22​(ζ)|2−1\displaystyle|a_{12}(\zeta)|^{2}\leq|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}-1≤|a22​(ζ)|2.\displaystyle\leq|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}.

We have‖A​(ζ)‖2=\displaystyle\|A(\zeta)\|^{2}=‖A​(ζ)∗​A​(ζ)‖≤tr​(A​(ζ)∗​A​(ζ))\displaystyle\|A(\zeta)^{*}A(\zeta)\|\leq\text{\rm tr}(A(\zeta)^{*}A(\zeta))=\displaystyle=|a11​(ζ)|2+|a12​(ζ)|2+|a21​(ζ)|2+|a22​(ζ)|2≤4​|a22​(ζ)|2.\displaystyle|a_{11}(\zeta)|^{2}+|a_{12}(\zeta)|^{2}+|a_{21}(\zeta)|^{2}+|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}\leq 4|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}.(A.4)

On the other handγ22​(ζ)+1=|a22​(ζ)|2−|a21​(ζ)|2=|a22​(ζ)|2​(1−|s​(ζ)|2),s​(ζ)=a21​(ζ)a22​(ζ).\gamma_{22}(\zeta)+1=|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}-|a_{21}(\zeta)|^{2}=|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}(1-|s(\zeta)|^{2}),\ \ s(\zeta)=\frac{a_{21}(\zeta)}{a_{22}(\zeta)}.

The functions​(ζ)s(\zeta)belongs to the Schur class. Sinces​(0)=0s(0)=0, by Schwarz lemma|s​(ζ)|2≤|ζ|2|s(\zeta)|^{2}\leq|\zeta|^{2}.
Thuslog⁡|a22​(ζ)|2+log⁡(1−|ζ|2)≤log⁡(γ22​(ζ)+1).\log|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}+\log(1-|\zeta|^{2})\leq\log(\gamma_{22}(\zeta)+1).

On the imaginary axisz=i​yz=iywe haveζ=|ζ|\zeta=|\zeta|, consequently11−|ζ|2≤(1+|ζ|)21−|ζ|2=y.\frac{1}{1-|\zeta|^{2}}\leq\frac{(1+|\zeta|)^{2}}{1-|\zeta|^{2}}=y.

Thereforelog⁡|a22​(ζ)|2≤log⁡(γ22​(ζ)+1)+log⁡y.\log|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}\leq\log(\gamma_{22}(\zeta)+1)+\log y.

In combination with
(A.4) we obtainlim supy→∞log⁡‖𝔄​(i​y)‖M​(i​y)=lim supy→∞log⁡‖A​(ζ)‖M​(i​y)≤lim supy→∞log⁡4​|a22​(ζ)|22​M​(i​y)\displaystyle\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log\|{\mathfrak{A}}(iy)\|}{M(iy)}=\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log\|A(\zeta)\|}{M(iy)}\leq\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log 4|a_{22}(\zeta)|^{2}}{2M(iy)}≤lim supy→∞log⁡4​y+log⁡(‖Γ​(ζ)‖+1)2​M​(i​y)=lim supy→∞log⁡‖Γ​(ζ)‖2​M​(i​y).\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log 4y+\log(\|\Gamma(\zeta)\|+1)}{2M(iy)}=\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log\|\Gamma(\zeta)\|}{2M(iy)}.(A.5)

Since‖Γ​(ζ)‖≤1+‖A​(ζ)‖2\|\Gamma(\zeta)\|\leq 1+\|A(\zeta)\|^{2}

the opposite estimationlim supy→∞log⁡‖Γ​(ζ)‖2​M​(i​y)≤lim supy→∞log⁡‖A​(ζ)‖M​(i​y)\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log\|\Gamma(\zeta)\|}{2M(iy)}\leq\limsup_{y\to\infty}\frac{\log\|A(\zeta)\|}{M(iy)}

is evident.
∎

## Corollary A.2.

For a monotonic chain𝒞={𝔄​(t,⋅)∣t∈[0,∞)}\mathscr{C}=\{{\mathfrak{A}}(t,\cdot)\mid t\in[0,\infty)\}theMM-typeℓ​(t)\ell(t), whenever it is well defined, is monotonic.

## Proof.

Monotonicity of the chain means that𝔄​(z,t1)​J​𝔄​(z,t1)∗−Jz−z¯≤𝔄​(z,t2)​J​𝔄​(z,t2)∗−Jz−z¯,t1<t2.\frac{{\mathfrak{A}}(z,t_{1})J{\mathfrak{A}}(z,t_{1})^{*}-J}{z-\overline{z}}\leq\frac{{\mathfrak{A}}(z,t_{2})J{\mathfrak{A}}(z,t_{2})^{*}-J}{z-\overline{z}},\quad t_{1}<t_{2}.

Therefore‖𝔄​(z,t1)​J​𝔄​(z,t1)∗−J‖≤‖𝔄​(z,t2)​J​𝔄​(z,t2)∗−J‖,t1<t2.\|{\mathfrak{A}}(z,t_{1})J{\mathfrak{A}}(z,t_{1})^{*}-J\|\leq\|{\mathfrak{A}}(z,t_{2})J{\mathfrak{A}}(z,t_{2})^{*}-J\|,\quad t_{1}<t_{2}.

Then (A.2) impliesℓ​(t1)≤ℓ​(t2)\ell(t_{1})\leq\ell(t_{2}).
∎

## Remark A.3.

The norm of an individual𝔧{\mathfrak{j}}-expanding matrixAAcan not be estimated in terms of itsjj-formΓ\Gamma, sinceΓ\Gammadoes not depend on a possible𝔧{\mathfrak{j}}-unitary multiplier ofAAfrom the right. Note that the norm of a𝔧{\mathfrak{j}}-unitary matrix can be arbitrary large. However, as soon as we have a matrix functionA​(ζ)A(\zeta)it is enough to normalize it at a single point, sayζ=0\zeta=0, to have an estimation‖A​(ζ)‖\|A(\zeta)\|by‖Γ​(ζ)‖\|\Gamma(\zeta)\|in an arbitrary pointζ∈𝔻\zeta\in{\mathbb{D}}. To the purpose of such normalization, V.P. Potapov introduce a concept of𝔧{\mathfrak{j}}-modulus. The Arov normalization is much simpler and provides similar or better estimations. It appeared naturally in the theory of unitary extensions of isometries. For details see the appendices in[22].
